Experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of RealGeneral in multiple visual generation tasks, showing improved subject similarity in customized generation and image quality in canny-to-image tasks, while also reducing the training data required compared to existing unified models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper presents a relatively novel approach to unifying image generation tasks by leveraging pre-trained video models. The analogy to in-context learning in LLMs is a compelling one, providing a conceptual foundation for the work.  The introduction of UCE and the modified DiT block with SC-AdaLN and FCD are also novel contributions. The idea of using video models as a foundation for various image generation tasks is not entirely new, but RealGeneral's approach and specific architectural components make it a significant step in this direction.  There might be an overlap with some concurrent work (e.g., UniReal), but the authors do a good job of outlining the difference in motivation and design choice.

**Significance:**

The significance of the paper stems from its potential to simplify and improve image generation by reducing the need for task-specific architectures and large, specialized datasets. The ability to leverage the temporal coherence learned by video models for single-image generation is a promising avenue.  The demonstrated improvements in subject similarity and image quality are also significant. Moreover, the reduced training data requirement of fine-tuning the pretrained video model compared to training from scratch is important for practical applications.  The work could inspire more research into exploiting video models for diverse visual tasks and encourage the development of more generalizable image generation systems.

**Strengths:**

*   **Conceptual Clarity:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of unifying image generation and provides a convincing argument for using video models.
*   **Technical Contributions:** The proposed UCE and modified DiT block (SC-AdaLN, FCD) are well-designed and address specific challenges in multi-modal image generation.
*   **Experimental Validation:** The paper presents a thorough experimental evaluation across multiple tasks, comparing RealGeneral to relevant baselines and demonstrating its effectiveness.
*   **Reduced Data Requirement:**  The use of fine-tuning instead of training from scratch significantly reduces data requirements.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Reliance on Pre-trained Video Models:** The performance of RealGeneral is heavily dependent on the quality and generalizability of the underlying pre-trained video model.  A limitation would be if this approach does not extend to other pre-trained video models.
*   **Limited Scope of Tasks:** While the paper evaluates RealGeneral on three tasks, it would be beneficial to assess its performance on a wider range of image generation tasks to demonstrate its true versatility.
*   **Complexity:** The UCE, SC-AdaLN and FCD add complexity to the model, it's not fully clear how each of those modules contribute toward the final results. More controlled experiments would highlight each individual module's contribution.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of **7**.

*   **Novelty (2/3):**  The core idea of using video models for unified image generation has been explored before, but the specific approach of formulating the problem as temporal in-context learning along with the proposed architectural modifications demonstrates sufficient novelty.
*   **Significance (3/4):** The potential for simplified and improved image generation is significant. It can reduce data dependencies and training costs while simultaneously improving performance, making it a valuable step toward truly generalizable models.
*   **Experiments (2/3):**  The experimental evaluation is thorough, but I would have appreciated a broader range of tasks or more extensive ablation studies.

The paper presents a compelling and promising approach to unifying image generation, but it's not without limitations. The novelty is solid, the potential impact is high, and the experimental results are convincing. However, a more comprehensive evaluation and further analysis of the individual module contributions would strengthen the work. The heavy reliance on a pre-trained model also adds a layer of dependence, decreasing the score.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Understanding the Logical Capabilities of Large Language Models via Out-of-Context Representation Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10408v1)
- **Authors**: Jonathan Shaki, Emanuele La Malfa, Michael Wooldridge, Sarit Kraus
- **Abstract**: We study the capabilities of Large Language Models (LLM) on binary relations, a ubiquitous concept in math employed in most reasoning, math and logic benchmarks. This work focuses on equality, inequality, and inclusion, along with the properties they satisfy, such as ir/reflexivity, a/symmetry, transitivity, and logical complexity (e.g., number of reasoning ``hops''). We propose an alternative to in-context learning that trains only the representations of newly introduced tokens, namely out-of-context representation learning. This method mitigates linguistic biases already present in a model and, differently from in-context learning, does not rely on external information or illustrations. We argue out-of-context representation learning as a better alternative to in-context learning and fine-tuning to evaluate the capabilities of LLMs on logic tasks that are the building blocks of more complex reasoning benchmarks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary**

The paper investigates the ability of Large Language Models (LLMs) to reason about binary relations (equality, inequality, inclusion) and their properties (reflexivity, symmetry, transitivity) in an *out-of-context* setting.  Instead of using in-context learning or fine-tuning, the authors propose a novel *out-of-context representation learning* technique. This involves adding new tokens to the model's vocabulary to represent the elements of the relations and training *only* the embeddings of these new tokens, leaving the original model parameters unchanged.  The goal is to isolate and evaluate the inherent relational reasoning capabilities of the LLM, avoiding biases introduced by pre-existing token semantics or explicit examples provided during training or inference. The experiments are conducted on Llama-3-8B and Mistral-7B-v0.3, comparing the out-of-context representation learning with in-context learning.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Novelty:** The *out-of-context representation learning* approach is the most novel aspect of the paper. While fine-tuning and in-context learning are common techniques, this method of training *only* new token embeddings provides a cleaner way to assess a model's intrinsic reasoning capabilities about relations. This is a useful contribution that helps to isolate inherent reasoning abilities from biases resulting from model training or in-context examples.

*   **Significance:** Understanding the intrinsic reasoning capabilities of LLMs regarding fundamental logical concepts like binary relations is crucial. These relations are building blocks for more complex reasoning tasks, math problems, and multi-agent scenarios. The paper addresses the question of how well LLMs reason about these relations without the crutch of in-context examples, giving more insight into the inherent capability. It also addresses the question of whether linguistic confounds limit our ability to infer logical competence from LLM performance. These findings have significant implications for how LLMs are evaluated and how their reasoning abilities can be enhanced. The observation regarding a "distance effect," mirroring human cognition, also adds an interesting dimension.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The *methodology* is well-defined and presents a clear alternative to traditional fine-tuning and in-context learning for evaluating LLMs' logical reasoning.
    *   The *experiments* cover a range of binary relations and their properties, providing a relatively comprehensive analysis.
    *   The *analysis of the learned representations*, including the PCA visualizations, provides insights into how LLMs encode these relations internally.
    *   The *discussion* highlights the limitations of existing techniques and presents interesting observations such as the distance effect and provides some interesting comparisons to work on human cognition.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The *results* are somewhat mixed. While the illustrative information setting performs better, the models often struggle or provide inconclusive results in the minimal sufficient hypotheses setting. This suggests that LLMs need more than just the bare minimum information to generalize effectively. This is a common issue with LLMs, and thus somewhat detracts from the value of the experiment in the minimal sufficient hypotheses.
    *   The *models* tested (Llama-3-8B and Mistral-7B-v0.3) are relatively small compared to the largest available LLMs. While using smaller models makes the experiments more tractable, it limits the generalizability of the results to state-of-the-art LLMs.
    *   The *experimental setting* appears simplistic. It would be interesting to investigate the same with some more complex and practical problems to demonstrate real-world utility.
    * The claims made related to the relationship between the number of reasoning steps and performance is rather weak and unpersuasive.
    * The study is heavily reliant on statistical significance tests to compare results and the results are very close. The study would be enhanced by showing results that exhibit more significant differences.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper presents a useful method for probing the capabilities of LLMs. This approach could be adopted by other researchers to evaluate different aspects of LLM reasoning or to compare the effectiveness of different training techniques. The insights gained from analyzing the learned representations could also inform the development of more robust and reliable LLMs.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper makes a valuable methodological contribution in terms of an alternate approach to evaluating logical reasoning ability. However, the limited scope in terms of model size, rather simplistic problem domain, somewhat mixed results, and lack of real-world examples prevent it from receiving a higher score. The claims that relate to parallels with observations in human cognition are not compelling.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### BeamLLM: Vision-Empowered mmWave Beam Prediction with Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10432v1)
- **Authors**: Can Zheng, Jiguang He, Guofa Cai, Zitong Yu, Chung G. Kang
- **Abstract**: In this paper, we propose BeamLLM, a vision-aided millimeter-wave (mmWave) beam prediction framework leveraging large language models (LLMs) to address the challenges of high training overhead and latency in mmWave communication systems. By combining computer vision (CV) with LLMs' cross-modal reasoning capabilities, the framework extracts user equipment (UE) positional features from RGB images and aligns visual-temporal features with LLMs' semantic space through reprogramming techniques. Evaluated on a realistic vehicle-to-infrastructure (V2I) scenario, the proposed method achieves 61.01% top-1 accuracy and 97.39% top-3 accuracy in standard prediction tasks, significantly outperforming traditional deep learning models. In few-shot prediction scenarios, the performance degradation is limited to 12.56% (top-1) and 5.55% (top-3) from time sample 1 to 10, demonstrating superior prediction capability.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes BeamLLM, a framework that uses large language models (LLMs) for millimeter-wave (mmWave) beam prediction, incorporating computer vision (CV).  BeamLLM aims to overcome the challenges of high training overhead and latency in mmWave communication by leveraging LLMs' cross-modal reasoning capabilities. It extracts user equipment (UE) position features from RGB images and aligns visual-temporal features with LLMs using reprogramming techniques.  Experiments in a vehicle-to-infrastructure (V2I) scenario demonstrate that BeamLLM outperforms traditional deep learning models in standard prediction tasks and exhibits robust performance in few-shot prediction scenarios.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Novel Approach:** Integrating LLMs with computer vision for mmWave beam prediction is a relatively novel idea. Using visual features (RGB images) as input for beam selection, instead of relying on historical beam indices or AoD information, presents a different perspective from prior works.
    *   **Practical Focus:** The paper validates the framework using a real-world measurement dataset (DeepSense 6G), making it more relevant for practical deployment than purely theoretical studies.
    *   **Few-Shot Performance:** The paper highlights BeamLLM's robustness in few-shot learning, a key advantage for reducing the reliance on extensive training data. This is especially relevant for dynamic mmWave environments where retraining is frequent.
    *   **Clear Methodology:** The paper provides a detailed description of the BeamLLM architecture, including the visual feature extraction module, the backbone module, and the reprogramming techniques. The explanation of patch reprogramming and prompt-as-prefix (PaP) is clear and understandable.
    *   **Performance Improvement:** The results clearly show BeamLLM outperforming traditional DL models (RNN, GRU, LSTM) in both standard and few-shot prediction tasks. The ablation study effectively demonstrates the contribution of the PaP module.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **High Complexity and Computational Cost:** The paper acknowledges that using LLMs increases inference complexity and resource consumption. This is a major drawback, as real-time beam prediction is crucial in mmWave systems. While few-shot learning mitigates the training data requirement, the computational cost for online inference is a bottleneck for LLMs.
    *   **Limited Novelty in LLM Technique:** While the application is novel, the specific LLM techniques used (reprogramming, prompt engineering) aren't groundbreaking in the general NLP/LLM field. The approach is essentially adapting existing LLM techniques to a new domain.
    *   **Frozen Backbone Model:** Keeping the LLM backbone model frozen limits the framework's ability to adapt to specific mmWave channel characteristics and visual environments. Fine-tuning could potentially lead to even better performance. The large number of trainable parameters shows that even with the backbone frozen the model has a high capacity.
    *   **Dependency on Visual Clarity:** The framework relies on the quality of the RGB images. Performance could degrade significantly in poor visibility conditions (e.g., fog, rain, darkness), which is a limitation not thoroughly addressed.
    *   **Limited Baselines:** Comparing against other existing vision-aided beam prediction techniques might have strengthened the contribution. The compared baselines are primarily time-series prediction models and don't fully address the impact of visual information.

*   **Significance:**

    The paper's significance lies in demonstrating the *potential* of LLMs for mmWave beam prediction, particularly in scenarios with limited data. It opens up a new avenue for research by combining CV and LLMs in wireless communication. However, the practical applicability of BeamLLM is limited by the high computational cost and dependency on clear visual conditions. Further research is needed to address these limitations and optimize the framework for real-world deployments.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the strengths and weaknesses, and the novelty of applying existing LLM techniques to a new domain I believe a score of 6 is appropriate.

*   The paper introduces a novel application of LLMs.
*   The experimental results demonstrate a clear performance improvement over traditional methods, particularly in few-shot scenarios.
*   It validates the framework using a real-world dataset.

However, the paper also suffers from significant limitations:

*   High computational complexity, limiting practicality.
*   Limited novelty in terms of LLM techniques.
*   Dependency on visual data quality.
*   The performance gain appears to be largely dependent on existing LLM components like the reprogrammed LLM and PaP, thus highlighting the importance of components outside of the specific engineering/wireless contribution.

### 4D LangSplat: 4D Language Gaussian Splatting via Multimodal Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10437v1)
- **Authors**: Wanhua Li, Renping Zhou, Jiawei Zhou, Yingwei Song, Johannes Herter, Minghan Qin, Gao Huang, Hanspeter Pfister
- **Abstract**: Learning 4D language fields to enable time-sensitive, open-ended language queries in dynamic scenes is essential for many real-world applications. While LangSplat successfully grounds CLIP features into 3D Gaussian representations, achieving precision and efficiency in 3D static scenes, it lacks the ability to handle dynamic 4D fields as CLIP, designed for static image-text tasks, cannot capture temporal dynamics in videos. Real-world environments are inherently dynamic, with object semantics evolving over time. Building a precise 4D language field necessitates obtaining pixel-aligned, object-wise video features, which current vision models struggle to achieve. To address these challenges, we propose 4D LangSplat, which learns 4D language fields to handle time-agnostic or time-sensitive open-vocabulary queries in dynamic scenes efficiently. 4D LangSplat bypasses learning the language field from vision features and instead learns directly from text generated from object-wise video captions via Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs). Specifically, we propose a multimodal object-wise video prompting method, consisting of visual and text prompts that guide MLLMs to generate detailed, temporally consistent, high-quality captions for objects throughout a video. These captions are encoded using a Large Language Model into high-quality sentence embeddings, which then serve as pixel-aligned, object-specific feature supervision, facilitating open-vocabulary text queries through shared embedding spaces. Recognizing that objects in 4D scenes exhibit smooth transitions across states, we further propose a status deformable network to model these continuous changes over time effectively. Our results across multiple benchmarks demonstrate that 4D LangSplat attains precise and efficient results for both time-sensitive and time-agnostic open-vocabulary queries.
- **Summary**: Okay, I'll provide a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper, including a novelty/significance score.

**Summary:**

The paper "4D LangSplat: 4D Language Gaussian Splatting via Multimodal Large Language Models" addresses the problem of enabling time-sensitive, open-ended language queries in dynamic 3D scenes.  The authors propose 4D LangSplat, a novel approach that extends the existing LangSplat method by incorporating a time-varying semantic field. Instead of relying on vision features to capture dynamic semantics (which CLIP struggles with), 4D LangSplat leverages Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) to generate detailed, temporally consistent object-wise video captions. These captions are then encoded using an LLM into sentence embeddings, providing pixel-aligned, object-specific supervision for the 4D language field.  Furthermore, the paper introduces a "status deformable network" to model the smooth transitions between object states over time, enhancing temporal consistency. The authors demonstrate improved performance on several benchmarks for both time-agnostic and time-sensitive open-vocabulary queries.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Novel Approach:** The core idea of using MLLMs to generate object-wise captions for dynamic scenes to supervise a 4D language field is genuinely novel.  It directly circumvents the limitations of using static image-text alignment methods like CLIP in dynamic environments.
    *   **Technically Sound:** The combination of 4D Gaussian Splatting, MLLM-based captioning, and a status deformable network is technically well-executed. The status deformable network seems a crucial addition to ensure temporal coherence.
    *   **Strong Experimental Results:** The experiments comprehensively demonstrate the effectiveness of 4D LangSplat, showing improvements over existing methods across multiple datasets and query types. Ablation studies help validate the contributions of individual components. The visualization of the temporal results is convincing.
    *   **Addressing a Relevant Problem:** Enabling language queries in dynamic 3D scenes is a vital area, with growing applications in robotics, scene understanding, and interactive environments. This work directly tackles a previously underexplored facet of this challenge.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on MLLM Quality:** The performance of 4D LangSplat is inherently tied to the quality of the MLLM-generated captions. While the authors use a strong MLLM, any biases or inaccuracies in the MLLM will propagate through the pipeline. The MLLM dependency also inherently limits the representation by the capacity of the utilized models, though the authors recognize this in the discussion.
    *   **Computational Cost:** While the paper claims efficiency, the use of MLLMs for caption generation is computationally expensive. The authors do not thoroughly address the computational overhead. This might limit the scalability of the approach, though they have been showing significant increases in this area. The FPS analysis is helpful in comparing their method's runtime to alternatives, but more in-depth discussion of compute time would be beneficial.
    *   **Limited Generalization Analysis:** While multiple datasets are used, it would be good to see how it generalizes to different types of dynamic scenes, especially more complex real-world environments.
    *   **Annotation Reliance:** The method relies on robust and temporally consistent SAM and DEVA tracking for generating the objects masks. Any inaccuracies or inconsistencies in the masks can significantly affect the quality of the generated captions. This point, while a dependency on external tools rather than a core weakness of the method, is a significant practical consideration.
    *   **Incremental Improvement?** While there is a good improvement over LangSplat, how significantly does the entire pipeline outperform the LangSplat method? Given all the extra added features, is it a significant enough improvement to be considered a significant step forward?

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    The paper presents a significant advance in the field of 3D language fields. The key innovation lies in using MLLMs for generating temporal captions to supervise the learning of a 4D language field. This represents a paradigm shift away from relying solely on vision-based features, addressing a critical limitation of existing methods. The introduction of the status deformable network further enhances the temporal coherence and realism of the results. The concept of object-aware video prompting and the generation of object-level features are novel and can be extended for further applications.

**Score:** 7.5

**Justification:**

The paper is novel and makes a significant contribution to the field of 3D language fields, addressing the critical challenge of time-sensitive queries in dynamic scenes. The idea of using MLLMs for captioning and the status deformable network are innovative and well-executed. The experimental results are strong.
The paper has minor weaknesses and is somewhat reliant on external tools like robust masks. The computation cost is a concern that might limit real-world deployability. Also, further testing might need to be done to show how the method generalizes to more complex real-world conditions.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Whisper Speaker Identification: Leveraging Pre-Trained Multilingual Transformers for Robust Speaker Embeddings
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10446v1)
- **Authors**: Jakaria Islam Emon, Md Abu Salek, Kazi Tamanna Alam
- **Abstract**: Speaker identification in multilingual settings presents unique challenges, particularly when conventional models are predominantly trained on English data. In this paper, we propose WSI (Whisper Speaker Identification), a framework that repurposes the encoder of the Whisper automatic speech recognition model pre trained on extensive multilingual data to generate robust speaker embeddings via a joint loss optimization strategy that leverages online hard triplet mining and self supervised Normalized Temperature-scaled Cross Entropy loss. By capitalizing on Whisper language-agnostic acoustic representations, our approach effectively distinguishes speakers across diverse languages and recording conditions. Extensive evaluations on multiple corpora, including VoxTube (multilingual), JVS (Japanese), CallHome (German, Spanish, Chinese, and Japanese), and Voxconverse (English), demonstrate that WSI consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines, namely Pyannote Embedding, ECAPA TDNN, and Xvector, in terms of lower equal error rates and higher AUC scores. These results validate our hypothesis that a multilingual pre-trained ASR encoder, combined with joint loss optimization, substantially improves speaker identification performance in non-English languages.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces WSI (Whisper Speaker Identification), a framework that leverages the pre-trained encoder of the Whisper ASR model to generate robust speaker embeddings for multilingual speaker identification. WSI employs a joint loss optimization strategy combining online hard triplet mining and self-supervised NT-Xent loss. By utilizing Whisper's language-agnostic acoustic representations, the framework aims to effectively distinguish speakers across various languages and recording conditions.  The authors demonstrate the efficacy of WSI through evaluations on multilingual corpora such as VoxTube, JVS, CallHome, and Voxconverse, showing improved performance compared to state-of-the-art baselines (Pyannote, ECAPA-TDNN, X-vector) in terms of lower equal error rates and higher AUC scores.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the repurposing of a pre-trained *multilingual* ASR encoder (Whisper) for speaker identification, specifically focusing on multilingual scenarios. While using pre-trained models for speaker tasks is not entirely new, the emphasis on multilingual robustness and the joint loss optimization using both triplet loss and self-supervised NT-Xent makes this contribution moderately novel.  The use of online hard triplet mining is also a well-established technique, but its combination with Whisper's architecture and the NT-Xent loss is specific to this paper.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial challenge in speaker recognition: performance degradation in multilingual environments, a common problem when models are primarily trained on a single language like English. The demonstrated ability of WSI to consistently outperform baselines across diverse languages highlights its practical significance. Improved speaker identification has applications in various domains, including diarization, security, and personalized speech applications.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Comprehensive evaluation across multiple multilingual datasets.
    *   Clear articulation of the problem and proposed solution.
    *   Significant performance gains over state-of-the-art baselines, particularly in multilingual settings.
    *   The use of joint loss optimization and data augmentation to enhance speaker discriminability is well-motivated.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The paper is somewhat incremental. While the combination of techniques is novel, the individual components are well-known. The novelty is in their integrated use specifically with the Whisper architecture.
    *   The paper doesn't deeply analyze *why* Whisper's embeddings are language-agnostic compared to other approaches. A more detailed explanation of the learned representations would add value. It implicitly attributes the result to the large, diverse training set Whisper was trained on.
    *   The experimental setup, while comprehensive, could benefit from ablation studies isolating the impact of each component (e.g., the contribution of the NT-Xent loss versus the triplet loss). The discussion of this ablation study in the final section is vague.
    *   The architecture itself leverages a relatively simple mean-pooling strategy after the encoder. More sophisticated pooling mechanisms might further enhance performance. The paper mentions exploring alternative strategies in the final section, without actually doing so.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper's findings could encourage further research into repurposing pre-trained ASR models for speaker-centric tasks. It demonstrates the potential of leveraging large, multilingual ASR models as a feature extractor in speaker recognition. The combined loss optimization approach could also inspire further investigations into multi-task learning for speaker identification.

**Score:** 6

**Justification:**

A score of 6 reflects the paper's solid contribution to the field while acknowledging its incremental nature and areas for improvement. The repurposing of Whisper is a valuable finding, but the method combines existing techniques in a specific way.  The multilingual performance boost is significant and demonstrates clear practical value. The lack of more detailed analysis of the learned representations and more comprehensive ablation studies prevent the paper from scoring higher. The practical benefit and sound methodology warrant a higher score than a purely incremental contribution, but the lack of more fundamental insight lowers the score.

- **Classification**: cs.SD
- **Score**: 6/10

### DynaCode: A Dynamic Complexity-Aware Code Benchmark for Evaluating Large Language Models in Code Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10452v1)
- **Authors**: Wenhao Hu, Jinhao Duan, Chunchen Wei, Li Zhang, Yue Zhang, Kaidi Xu
- **Abstract**: The rapid advancement of large language models (LLMs) has significantly improved their performance in code generation tasks. However, existing code benchmarks remain static, consisting of fixed datasets with predefined problems. This makes them vulnerable to memorization during training, where LLMs recall specific test cases instead of generalizing to new problems, leading to data contamination and unreliable evaluation results. To address these issues, we introduce DynaCode, a dynamic, complexity-aware benchmark that overcomes the limitations of static datasets. DynaCode evaluates LLMs systematically using a complexity-aware metric, incorporating both code complexity and call-graph structures. DynaCode achieves large-scale diversity, generating up to 189 million unique nested code problems across four distinct levels of code complexity, referred to as units, and 16 types of call graphs. Results on 12 latest LLMs show an average performance drop of 16.8% to 45.7% compared to MBPP+, a static code generation benchmark, with performance progressively decreasing as complexity increases. This demonstrates DynaCode's ability to effectively differentiate LLMs. Additionally, by leveraging call graphs, we gain insights into LLM behavior, particularly their preference for handling subfunction interactions within nested code.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the "DynaCode: A Dynamic Complexity-Aware Code Benchmark for Evaluating Large Language Models in Code Generation" paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces DynaCode, a new dynamic benchmark for evaluating large language models (LLMs) in code generation.  DynaCode addresses limitations of static benchmarks, specifically data contamination and lack of complexity control. It dynamically generates Python code problems, categorizing them based on code complexity (using cyclomatic complexity) and constructing nested problems through call graphs.  This creates a more diverse and challenging evaluation environment. The authors evaluate several recent LLMs using DynaCode, revealing a significant performance drop compared to static benchmarks like MBPP+. The analysis also provides insights into LLM behavior concerning call graph structures and function dependencies.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core concept of a dynamic and complexity-aware benchmark is valuable and addresses a recognized problem with existing static code generation benchmarks. The use of both code complexity metrics (cyclomatic complexity) *and* call graph structure to generate increasingly difficult test cases is a significant strength. Many prior works focus on one or the other. The automatic generation of these nested code problems with validated I/O pairs further adds to the novelty.

*   **Significance:** The paper demonstrates that DynaCode can effectively differentiate LLM performance, uncovering weaknesses that static benchmarks might miss due to memorization or insufficient complexity. The benchmark's design, which incorporates both code complexity and call-graph complexity, offers insights into LLM capabilities regarding handling nested code, function dependencies, and complex execution flows. This has practical implications for developing and selecting LLMs for real-world coding tasks. The analysis of error types provides further diagnostic information that goes beyond a simple pass/fail score. The mitigation of data contamination is a key strength, ensuring the results better reflect true generalization ability.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Addresses a clear and relevant problem (data contamination and limited complexity in static benchmarks).
    *   The dynamic generation of problems and complexity-aware metrics offer a more realistic evaluation of LLMs.
    *   The use of call graphs adds a unique dimension to the evaluation, providing insights into how LLMs handle function dependencies.
    *   The experimental results convincingly demonstrate that DynaCode is more effective at differentiating LLM performance than MBPP and MBPP+.
    *   The paper presents clear error analysis revealing types of failures and thereby helping better understand LLM capabilities and limitations.
    *   The inclusion of both a commercial and an open-source LLM helps to broaden the impact of the findings.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The reliance on cyclomatic complexity as the sole measure of code complexity has limitations. Other metrics, such as Halstead's metrics or more advanced complexity measures, could potentially be incorporated for a more comprehensive assessment.
    *   The call graph structures, while well-defined, are relatively simple (maximum of 5 nodes). More complex structures might be necessary to fully challenge future LLMs.
    *   The paper could benefit from a more in-depth discussion of the limitations of the dynamic generation process. How does the generation process impact the diversity of the test suite? Are there inherent biases in the kinds of problems that can be generated?
    *   The prompt engineering could be described in more detail. What strategies were considered and why was the presented strategy the most effective?

*   **Potential Influence:** DynaCode has the potential to become a widely used benchmark for evaluating LLMs in code generation. Its dynamic nature and complexity-aware metrics make it a valuable tool for researchers and practitioners. It promotes the development of LLMs that are not only capable of generating correct code but also of handling complex and realistic coding tasks.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a well-designed, novel, and significant contribution to the field of code generation evaluation. It rigorously addresses known limitations of static benchmarks by proposing a dynamic, complexity-aware approach that offers a more realistic and informative assessment of LLM performance. While there are some limitations related to the choice of complexity metrics and call graph structure complexity, the strengths of the paper outweigh these weaknesses. It's likely to become a widely used benchmark. Therefore:

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Sentiment Analysis in SemEval: A Review of Sentiment Identification Approaches
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10457v1)
- **Authors**: Bousselham El Haddaoui, Raddouane Chiheb, Rdouan Faizi, Abdellatif El Afia
- **Abstract**: Social media platforms are becoming the foundations of social interactions including messaging and opinion expression. In this regard, Sentiment Analysis techniques focus on providing solutions to ensure the retrieval and analysis of generated data including sentiments, emotions, and discussed topics. International competitions such as the International Workshop on Semantic Evaluation (SemEval) have attracted many researchers and practitioners with a special research interest in building sentiment analysis systems. In our work, we study top-ranking systems for each SemEval edition during the 2013-2021 period, a total of 658 teams participated in these editions with increasing interest over years. We analyze the proposed systems marking the evolution of research trends with a focus on the main components of sentiment analysis systems including data acquisition, preprocessing, and classification. Our study shows an active use of preprocessing techniques, an evolution of features engineering and word representation from lexicon-based approaches to word embeddings, and the dominance of neural networks and transformers over the classification phase fostering the use of ready-to-use models. Moreover, we provide researchers with insights based on experimented systems which will allow rapid prototyping of new systems and help practitioners build for future SemEval editions.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a review of sentiment analysis systems participating in the SemEval competition from 2013 to 2021.  It analyzes top-performing systems across these editions, focusing on key components like data acquisition, preprocessing, feature engineering, and classification models. The paper aims to track the evolution of techniques used, highlight opportunities and limitations of different approaches, and suggest future research directions in sentiment analysis. The authors analyze the trends in datasets used, preprocessing techniques, word representation methods (from lexicon-based to word embeddings), and the increasing dominance of neural networks and transformers for classification. The paper offers insights intended to help researchers rapidly prototype new systems and assist practitioners in building for future SemEval editions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Scope:** The paper covers a significant span of years (2013-2021) of SemEval competitions, providing a broad view of trends.
    *   **Structured Analysis:** The analysis is well-structured, breaking down sentiment analysis pipelines into distinct components, making it easier to understand the evolution of techniques.
    *   **Useful Insights:** The identified trends (e.g., the shift from lexicon-based to word embeddings, the rise of deep learning and transformers) are generally accurate and reflect the advancements in the field.
    *   **Clear Visualizations:** The graphs are helpful in visualizing trends, particularly the evolution of dataset sizes and classification model usage.
    *   **References:** The comprehensive list of references is valuable for researchers looking to delve deeper into specific aspects.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Novelty:** The paper is primarily a review. While reviews are valuable, this one doesn't present fundamentally new insights or frameworks. It summarizes known trends rather than offering novel analytical perspectives. The findings are largely expected.
    *   **Depth of Analysis:** While the paper identifies trends, the *depth* of analysis in certain areas could be greater. For example, a more critical assessment of the limitations of transformer models in specific SemEval tasks or a more nuanced discussion of different preprocessing choices would enhance the paper. The evaluation lacks critical thinking.
    *   **Subjectivity:** The "High/Average/Low" usage frequency categorization (e.g., for preprocessing techniques) is somewhat subjective. A more quantitative or objective metric for determining these categories would be preferable.
    *   **Limited Critical Discussion:** While the paper notes limitations of different approaches, a more critical *discussion* of *why* certain techniques performed better in specific SemEval tasks would add significant value. More depth on error analysis of approaches is required.
    *   **Impact:** The impact on the field is likely to be limited, as the paper primarily consolidates existing knowledge.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**
    * The paper lacks any significant novelty. It simply states information that is already present and available in the literature.
    * No new findings, research, or approach.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper is a competent review that summarizes the evolution of sentiment analysis techniques used in SemEval competitions. However, its primary weakness is the lack of significant novelty. It presents known trends without providing substantial new insights or critical perspectives that would significantly advance the field. The analysis, while well-structured, could be deeper and more critical.

Score: 5

The paper's comprehensiveness and structured analysis are valuable, pushing the score above a purely descriptive paper. However, the lack of originality and limited depth prevent a higher score.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 5/10

### LLMs in Disease Diagnosis: A Comparative Study of DeepSeek-R1 and O3 Mini Across Chronic Health Conditions
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10486v1)
- **Authors**: Gaurav Kumar Gupta, Pranal Pande
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are revolutionizing medical diagnostics by enhancing both disease classification and clinical decision-making. In this study, we evaluate the performance of two LLM- based diagnostic tools, DeepSeek R1 and O3 Mini, using a structured dataset of symptoms and diagnoses. We assessed their predictive accuracy at both the disease and category levels, as well as the reliability of their confidence scores. DeepSeek R1 achieved a disease-level accuracy of 76% and an overall accuracy of 82%, outperforming O3 Mini, which attained 72% and 75% respectively. Notably, DeepSeek R1 demonstrated exceptional performance in Mental Health, Neurological Disorders, and Oncology, where it reached 100% accuracy, while O3 Mini excelled in Autoimmune Disease classification with 100% accuracy. Both models, however, struggled with Respiratory Disease classification, recording accuracies of only 40% for DeepSeek R1 and 20% for O3 Mini. Additionally, the analysis of confidence scores revealed that DeepSeek R1 provided high-confidence predictions in 92% of cases, compared to 68% for O3 Mini. Ethical considerations regarding bias, model interpretability, and data privacy are also discussed to ensure the responsible integration of LLMs into clinical practice. Overall, our findings offer valuable insights into the strengths and limitations of LLM-based diagnostic systems and provide a roadmap for future enhancements in AI-driven healthcare.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary of the paper and a critical evaluation of its novelty and significance, followed by a score.

**Summary:**

The paper "LLMs in Disease Diagnosis: A Comparative Study of DeepSeek-R1 and O3 Mini Across Chronic Health Conditions" compares the performance of two large language models (LLMs), DeepSeek R1 and O3 Mini, in diagnosing chronic diseases based on symptom analysis. The authors evaluate the models' ability to classify diseases and predict specific disease names across different medical categories, also assessing the reliability of their confidence scores. DeepSeek R1 generally outperforms O3 Mini in overall accuracy, particularly in Mental Health, Neurological Disorders, and Oncology, while O3 Mini shows strength in Autoimmune Disease classification. Both models struggle with Respiratory Diseases. The paper discusses ethical considerations like bias and data privacy and limitations, aiming to contribute to the development of more reliable and ethically sound AI diagnostic tools.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Research Question:** The paper clearly defines its research question – to compare the performance of DeepSeek R1 and O3 Mini in chronic disease diagnosis.
    *   **Comparative Approach:** The direct comparison of two different LLMs is valuable, offering insights into their relative strengths and weaknesses.
    *   **Structured Methodology:** The study employs a structured methodology including well-defined evaluation metrics (Disease Level Accuracy, Category Level Accuracy, Overall Accuracy, and Confidence Score Distribution) and a standardized prompt for both LLMs.
    *   **Ethical Considerations:** The paper acknowledges and discusses ethical concerns such as bias and data privacy, which is crucial in medical AI.
    *   **Confidence Analysis:** Analysing the confidence scores produced by the models is a positive aspect of the work as it provides insight into the reliability of the model outputs.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Novelty:** The fundamental idea of using LLMs for disease diagnosis is not entirely novel. Numerous papers have explored this avenue using different models and datasets. The paper does add to this body of knowledge by explicitly comparing two models across chronic illnesses specifically. However, a comprehensive literature review positioning this work in light of relevant studies could be strengthened.
    *   **Data Limitations:** The dataset, while sourced from reputable medical websites, relies solely on symptoms, potentially oversimplifying real-world diagnostic scenarios where other data modalities (imaging, lab results) play a crucial role. The size and diversity of the dataset could also be a concern.
    *   **Lack of Real-World Validation:** The study lacks real-world validation with clinical data or clinicians' input. The results are based solely on simulated scenarios.
    *   **Limited Depth of Error Analysis:** While the paper identifies categories where models struggled (e.g., Respiratory Diseases), it lacks a deeper dive into the types of errors made and the specific reasons behind them.
    *   **Limited Explainability:** The paper mentions explainability as a future direction, but the current study does not explore techniques to improve the interpretability of the LLM's reasoning. While confidence scores are provided, the reasons behind these are not explained or analysed.
    *   **Generalisability:** Given the specific fine-tuning and characteristics of DeepSeek R1 and O3 Mini, the findings might not be directly generalizable to all LLMs or different medical diagnostic tasks.

*   **Significance:**
    *   The paper provides practical insights for healthcare professionals and AI developers interested in leveraging LLMs for disease diagnosis.
    *   It highlights the importance of considering specific medical domains when selecting and deploying LLMs, as different models may excel in different areas.
    *   The discussion of ethical considerations is timely and relevant, contributing to responsible AI development in healthcare.
    *   The findings can inform future research directions, such as exploring multi-modal data integration and developing more explainable AI diagnostic systems.

*   **Rationale for Score:**

The paper offers a valuable comparative analysis of two LLMs for chronic disease diagnosis, which contributes to the growing body of research in this area. However, the relatively limited novelty, data limitations, lack of real-world validation, and the absence of in-depth error analysis and explainability limit its overall impact and prevents it from achieving a higher score. While the insights are helpful and the methodology is sound, the paper doesn't break substantial new ground within the field.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Streaming Generation of Co-Speech Gestures via Accelerated Rolling Diffusion
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10488v1)
- **Authors**: Evgeniia Vu, Andrei Boiarov, Dmitry Vetrov
- **Abstract**: Generating co-speech gestures in real time requires both temporal coherence and efficient sampling. We introduce Accelerated Rolling Diffusion, a novel framework for streaming gesture generation that extends rolling diffusion models with structured progressive noise scheduling, enabling seamless long-sequence motion synthesis while preserving realism and diversity. We further propose Rolling Diffusion Ladder Acceleration (RDLA), a new approach that restructures the noise schedule into a stepwise ladder, allowing multiple frames to be denoised simultaneously. This significantly improves sampling efficiency while maintaining motion consistency, achieving up to a 2x speedup with high visual fidelity and temporal coherence. We evaluate our approach on ZEGGS and BEAT, strong benchmarks for real-world applicability. Our framework is universally applicable to any diffusion-based gesture generation model, transforming it into a streaming approach. Applied to three state-of-the-art methods, it consistently outperforms them, demonstrating its effectiveness as a generalizable and efficient solution for real-time, high-fidelity co-speech gesture synthesis.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Streaming Generation of Co-Speech Gestures via Accelerated Rolling Diffusion" introduces Accelerated Rolling Diffusion (ARD), a new framework for generating co-speech gestures in real-time. ARD extends rolling diffusion models using a structured progressive noise scheduling strategy. The approach also includes Rolling Diffusion Ladder Acceleration (RDLA), restructuring the noise schedule to denoise multiple frames simultaneously, improving sampling efficiency. The framework is designed to convert any diffusion-based gesture generation model into a streaming model without requiring post-processing. The authors validate their approach using the ZEGGS and BEAT datasets and user studies, demonstrating improvements in efficiency, realism, and robustness in real-time applications.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in adapting rolling diffusion models for the specific and practically important task of real-time co-speech gesture generation. While rolling diffusion itself isn't entirely new, its application to this streaming context, and the innovations introduced to accelerate and stabilize the process (RDLA, OFS, inertial loss), constitute a meaningful contribution. The "ladder acceleration" concept, while not a radical departure from existing noise scheduling techniques, is cleverly applied to enable parallel denoising and speed up inference. Converting any diffusion-based gesture generation approach into a real-time streaming model is also very novel.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical bottleneck in the deployment of generative AI for embodied agents and virtual avatars: the need for efficient, real-time motion synthesis. Overcoming the latency and discontinuities associated with traditional sliding window approaches is significant. The authors also conduct comprehensive experimental evaluations, with both quantitative metrics and user studies to demonstrate the advantages of their methods.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and well-motivated solution.
    *   The framework is generalizable, applicable to existing diffusion models.
    *   RDLA introduces a practical speedup with minimal quality degradation.
    *   Comprehensive experimental validation on standard benchmarks, user studies, and detailed ablation studies.
    *   Introduction of On-the-Fly Smoothing is practical.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the paper shows improvement against baseline diffusion models, the improvements in the user studies seems limited. Given the cost of the user studies, better user study design could have resulted in more noticeable difference.
    *   The description of some components, especially RDLA and OFS, could benefit from more visual aids and intuitive explanations. While the formulas are present, a diagrammatic representation would improve understanding.
    *   The work doesn't investigate other acceleration methods that is used in DDIM.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to influence the field of embodied AI and virtual avatars by enabling more responsive and interactive real-time experiences. The proposed framework could be adopted by researchers and practitioners working on virtual assistants, gaming, and telepresence systems. It provides a practical pathway for integrating diffusion-based motion synthesis into real-world applications.

*   **Rigorous Rationale:** The strengths of the paper, particularly its adaptation of rolling diffusion to real-time gesture generation, outweigh the weaknesses. The paper addresses a practically important problem and presents a well-validated solution that demonstrates a clear improvement over existing methods. There is room for further improvement in the experimental design, but the novelty and practical utility of the core framework warrant a solid score.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Source-primed Multi-turn Conversation Helps Large Language Models Translate Documents
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10494v1)
- **Authors**: Hanxu Hu, Jannis Vamvas, Rico Sennrich
- **Abstract**: LLMs have paved the way for truly simple document-level machine translation, but challenges such as omission errors remain. In this paper, we study a simple method for handling document-level machine translation, by leveraging previous contexts in a multi-turn conversational manner. Specifically, by decomposing documents into segments and iteratively translating them while maintaining previous turns, this method ensures coherent translations without additional training, and can fully re-use the KV cache of previous turns thus minimizing computational overhead. We further propose a `source-primed' method that first provides the whole source document before multi-turn translation. We empirically show this multi-turn method outperforms both translating entire documents in a single turn and translating each segment independently according to multiple automatic metrics in representative LLMs, establishing a strong baseline for document-level translation using LLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a "source-primed multi-turn" approach for document-level machine translation using large language models (LLMs). The method decomposes documents into segments and translates them iteratively in a conversational manner.  A key component is the initial "source priming" step where the entire source document is provided to the LLM *before* the segmented translation begins. This allows the model to consider future context, addressing a limitation of standard multi-turn translation. The authors empirically demonstrate that this multi-turn approach, particularly with source priming, outperforms single-turn and segment-level baselines using automatic metrics. They also analyze the impact of domain and document length.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

*   **Incremental Improvement:** The paper builds on existing work in multi-turn translation with LLMs. The novelty lies primarily in the addition of the initial "source priming" step. While this is a logical and potentially beneficial addition, its individual impact is not rigorously isolated or deeply explored. Other work has looked at document aware translation strategies (Jin, An and Ma, 2024).
*   **Limited Algorithmic Innovation:** The core approach is a straightforward application of LLM capabilities. There is no fundamentally new algorithm or training procedure presented. The work relies more on clever prompting strategies than deep algorithmic advancements.

**Significance:**

*   **Practical Relevance:** The multi-turn conversation strategy (with source priming) is easy to implement and can be readily adopted by practitioners using existing LLMs. This accessibility enhances its potential for real-world application.
*   **Strong Baseline:** The paper establishes a competitive baseline for document-level translation with LLMs, particularly when leveraging their conversational abilities. This can serve as a useful point of comparison for future research.
*   **Limited Analysis:** The analysis of the results is somewhat superficial. While the paper examines the impact of domain and document length, a deeper dive into the types of errors reduced (e.g., coherence, omissions, style) or the specific benefits conferred by source priming would strengthen the conclusions.
*   **Evaluation Concerns:** The paper relies heavily on automatic metrics (BLEU, COMET, BlonDE). While these metrics are standard, they are known to have limitations, particularly for assessing qualities like coherence and style in document-level translation.  Human evaluation is noted as an important future direction, suggesting an awareness of the limitations in evaluation. The results of the shared task comparison are useful, but a deeper analysis of the differences in model architecture and training data would be beneficial.
*   **Efficiency Considerations:** The authors claim efficiency benefits from caching attention keys/values. However, this is more of a theoretical argument than a demonstrated empirical advantage.  There's no specific analysis of the computational cost compared to single-turn approaches with very long contexts.

**Strengths:**

*   Simplicity and ease of implementation.
*   Demonstrated performance gains over baselines.
*   Addresses a practical problem (document-level MT).

**Weaknesses:**

*   Limited algorithmic novelty.
*   Superficial analysis of results.
*   Heavy reliance on automatic metrics.
*   Lack of empirical validation of efficiency claims.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper may influence researchers and practitioners to explore multi-turn conversational approaches and prompting strategies for LLMs in document-level machine translation. It could also serve as a baseline for comparing more complex document-level translation methods.

**Rigorous Rationale**

While the paper presents a useful and easily implementable technique, it lacks significant algorithmic innovation and relies on established LLM capabilities and automatic metrics. The added benefit of source priming over standard multi-turn approaches is not clearly established in the analysis, weakening the claimed novelty. This warrants a middling score.

Score: 5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 5/10

### MMLU-ProX: A Multilingual Benchmark for Advanced Large Language Model Evaluation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10497v1)
- **Authors**: Weihao Xuan, Rui Yang, Heli Qi, Qingcheng Zeng, Yunze Xiao, Yun Xing, Junjue Wang, Huitao Li, Xin Li, Kunyu Yu, Nan Liu, Qingyu Chen, Douglas Teodoro, Edison Marrese-Taylor, Shijian Lu, Yusuke Iwasawa, Yutaka Matsuo, Irene Li
- **Abstract**: Traditional benchmarks struggle to evaluate increasingly sophisticated language models in multilingual and culturally diverse contexts. To address this gap, we introduce MMLU-ProX, a comprehensive multilingual benchmark covering 13 typologically diverse languages with approximately 11,829 questions per language. Building on the challenging reasoning-focused design of MMLU-Pro, our framework employs a semi-automatic translation process: translations generated by state-of-the-art large language models (LLMs) are rigorously evaluated by expert annotators to ensure conceptual accuracy, terminological consistency, and cultural relevance. We comprehensively evaluate 25 state-of-the-art LLMs using 5-shot chain-of-thought (CoT) and zero-shot prompting strategies, analyzing their performance across linguistic and cultural boundaries. Our experiments reveal consistent performance degradation from high-resource languages to lower-resource ones, with the best models achieving over 70% accuracy on English but dropping to around 40% for languages like Swahili, highlighting persistent gaps in multilingual capabilities despite recent advances. MMLU-ProX is an ongoing project; we are expanding our benchmark by incorporating additional languages and evaluating more language models to provide a more comprehensive assessment of multilingual capabilities.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "MMLU-ProX: A Multilingual Benchmark for Advanced Large Language Model Evaluation":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MMLU-ProX, a new multilingual benchmark designed to evaluate the cross-lingual reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs). MMLU-ProX extends the existing MMLU-Pro benchmark to 13 typologically diverse languages. The benchmark consists of approximately 11,829 questions per language, translated using a semi-automatic pipeline involving LLM-generated translations followed by expert human verification to ensure accuracy, consistency, and cultural relevance. The paper evaluates 25 state-of-the-art LLMs using both 5-shot chain-of-thought (CoT) and zero-shot prompting strategies, analyzing performance across linguistic and cultural boundaries. The results reveal performance degradation from high-resource to low-resource languages, even in advanced models, highlighting persistent gaps in multilingual capabilities.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper's novelty lies in the creation of a *high-quality* multilingual benchmark using a *rigorous* translation and verification process that focuses on maintaining conceptual accuracy and cultural relevance. Existing multilingual benchmarks often rely on simpler machine translation methods, which can introduce errors and biases.  The semi-automatic translation pipeline with expert human validation is a significant improvement over purely machine-translated benchmarks. Furthermore, the paper focuses on extending a benchmark that's designed to evaluate reasoning, unlike many multilingual benchmarks focusing on translation or simpler tasks.

**Significance:**

The significance of MMLU-ProX stems from its ability to provide a more reliable and nuanced assessment of LLM cross-lingual reasoning abilities.  The benchmark helps identify performance gaps between languages, highlighting areas where models need further improvement. The insights gained from MMLU-ProX can guide the development of more equitable and globally accessible language technologies. The evaluation of state-of-the-art models provides valuable empirical data for researchers and practitioners.

**Strengths:**

*   **Rigorous Methodology:** The semi-automatic translation pipeline with human verification is a strong point, ensuring high-quality translations that preserve meaning and cultural relevance.
*   **Focus on Reasoning:**  Extending MMLU-Pro, which already emphasizes reasoning, to a multilingual setting allows for a focused assessment of cross-lingual reasoning abilities.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:**  The evaluation of 25 state-of-the-art LLMs provides a wide-ranging performance comparison and valuable data for the community.
*   **Clear Findings:**  The consistent observation of performance degradation from high-resource to low-resource languages is a significant finding that underscores the need for continued research in multilingual LLMs.
*   **Detailed Analysis:** The paper provides detailed analysis on the impact of prompting strategies and other parameters, offering insight into how to approach multilingual reasoning.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Language Coverage:** While 13 languages is a significant improvement, the world has many more. Expanding this benchmark in the future will increase its impact.
*   **Cost:** The extensive human verification is costly, which may limit the rate at which the benchmark can be updated with new languages or questions.
*   **Potential Bias:** Despite efforts to ensure cultural relevance, subtle biases may still exist in the questions or translations.
*   **Reliance on Existing Models for Translation:** Using current LLMs for the initial translation step can introduce biases from the models themselves.  If the model is bad at translating a specific concept, it will be consistently reproduced across the other models.

**Potential Influence:**

MMLU-ProX has the potential to become a widely used benchmark for evaluating LLMs in multilingual settings. It could drive research towards more equitable and globally accessible language technologies.  The rigorous methodology employed in its creation could also serve as a model for other multilingual benchmark development efforts. However, its ultimate influence will depend on how widely it is adopted and used by the research community, the scale in terms of language coverage and size, and how often the benchmark is updated.

**Score:** 8

**Justification:**

A score of 8 is justified because MMLU-ProX represents a significant advance in multilingual LLM evaluation, marked by its rigorous methodology, focus on reasoning, and the comprehensive evaluation of a number of models. While there's definitely room for growth, its high-quality dataset will push the field forward.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### TokenCarve: Information-Preserving Visual Token Compression in Multimodal Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10501v1)
- **Authors**: Xudong Tan, Peng Ye, Chongjun Tu, Jianjian Cao, Yaoxin Yang, Lin Zhang, Dongzhan Zhou, Tao Chen
- **Abstract**: Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) are becoming increasingly popular, while the high computational cost associated with multimodal data input, particularly from visual tokens, poses a significant challenge. Existing training-based token compression methods improve inference efficiency but require costly retraining, while training-free methods struggle to maintain performance when aggressively reducing token counts. In this study, we reveal that the performance degradation of MLLM closely correlates with the accelerated loss of information in the attention output matrix. This insight introduces a novel information-preserving perspective, making it possible to maintain performance even under extreme token compression. Based on this finding, we propose TokenCarve, a training-free, plug-and-play, two-stage token compression framework. The first stage employs an Information-Preservation-Guided Selection (IPGS) strategy to prune low-information tokens, while the second stage further leverages IPGS to guide token merging, minimizing information loss. Extensive experiments on 11 datasets and 2 model variants demonstrate the effectiveness of TokenCarve. It can even reduce the number of visual tokens to 22.2% of the original count, achieving a 1.23x speedup in inference, a 64% reduction in KV cache storage, and only a 1.54% drop in accuracy. Our code is available at https://github.com/ShawnTan86/TokenCarve.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "TokenCarve: Information-Preserving Visual Token Compression in Multimodal Large Language Models" addresses the computational cost of processing visual tokens in MLLMs. The authors observe a correlation between performance degradation in MLLMs and the accelerated loss of information in the attention output matrix when visual tokens are compressed.  Based on this, they propose TokenCarve, a training-free, plug-and-play, two-stage token compression framework. The first stage, Information-Preservation-Guided Selection (IPGS), prunes low-information tokens. The second stage merges tokens to minimize information loss, guided by IPGS.  The authors demonstrate the effectiveness of TokenCarve on various datasets and model variants, showing that it can significantly reduce the number of visual tokens with minimal performance impact and improved inference speed and KV cache storage.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its **information-preserving perspective** on visual token compression. While token compression is an established area, the authors offer a compelling argument for focusing on minimizing information loss in the attention matrix. This perspective guides the design of their two-stage TokenCarve framework. The IPGS strategy, leveraging SVD to quantify token contribution to overall information quantity, appears novel. The combination of this with attention scores is sensible, reflecting both information richness and cross-modal alignment.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance is substantial. MLLMs are gaining popularity, but their computational demands are a major bottleneck. The ability to reduce the number of visual tokens significantly without severely impacting performance has broad implications.
    *   The reported speedup, reduced KV cache usage, and near-lossless performance on several datasets are valuable.
    *   The training-free and plug-and-play nature of the method makes it practically appealing.
    *   The analysis provides a deeper understanding of the interaction between token count, information loss, and MLLM performance.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Strong Empirical Validation:** The paper presents extensive experiments on a wide variety of datasets and two model variants (7B and 13B) of LLaVA. This robust validation strengthens the credibility of the proposed method.
    *   **Clear Problem Definition and Motivation:** The paper clearly identifies the problem of high computational cost of MLLMs due to visual tokens and motivates the need for efficient compression techniques.
    *   **Insightful Analysis:** The correlation between MLLM performance and the information quantity in the attention output matrix is a key insight that drives the development of TokenCarve.
    *   **Well-Designed Method:** The two-stage approach is well-structured, with each stage addressing a specific aspect of token compression while preserving information.
    *   **Ablation Studies:** The ablation studies provide valuable insights into the contribution of different components of the proposed method.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope of Application:** The experiments are conducted primarily on the LLaVA architecture. While the results are promising, the generalizability of TokenCarve to other MLLM architectures is not fully explored.
    *   **Merge Proportion Sensitivity:** Although the authors claim the merge proportion has minimal impact, Figure 5 shows the performance varies within 1.5%. More rigorous analysis on this hyper-parameter might be useful.
    *   **Positional Encoding Optimization:** As the authors themselves acknowledge, the actual acceleration is limited by the positional encoding computation, which may necessitate further optimization.

*   **Potential Influence:** TokenCarve has the potential to influence the development of more efficient MLLMs. Its training-free approach and clear insights could be adopted and further developed by other researchers in the field.  The emphasis on information preservation could shift the focus of future research on token compression techniques.

**Justification for the Score:**

I assign a score of **8**. The paper's information-preserving approach to visual token compression is a significant contribution to the field of MLLMs. While the method is primarily validated on LLaVA, the core insights and techniques hold promise for wider applicability. The clear problem definition, well-designed method, strong empirical results, and the training-free and plug-and-play nature make it highly valuable. The limitations regarding positional encoding optimization and merge proportion warrants not giving a higher rating. Overall, the paper offers a compelling solution to a critical problem in MLLMs and represents a valuable contribution.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### SySLLM: Generating Synthesized Policy Summaries for Reinforcement Learning Agents Using Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10509v1)
- **Authors**: Sahar Admoni, Omer Ben-Porat, Ofra Amir
- **Abstract**: Policies generated by Reinforcement Learning (RL) algorithms can be difficult to describe to users, as they result from the interplay between complex reward structures and neural network-based representations. This combination often leads to unpredictable behaviors, making policies challenging to analyze and posing significant obstacles to fostering human trust in real-world applications. Global policy summarization methods aim to describe agent behavior through a demonstration of actions in a subset of world-states. However, users can only watch a limited number of demonstrations, restricting their understanding of policies. Moreover, those methods overly rely on user interpretation, as they do not synthesize observations into coherent patterns. In this work, we present SySLLM (Synthesized Summary using LLMs), a novel method that employs synthesis summarization, utilizing large language models' (LLMs) extensive world knowledge and ability to capture patterns, to generate textual summaries of policies. Specifically, an expert evaluation demonstrates that the proposed approach generates summaries that capture the main insights generated by experts while not resulting in significant hallucinations. Additionally, a user study shows that SySLLM summaries are preferred over demonstration-based policy summaries and match or surpass their performance in objective agent identification tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SySLLM, a novel method that leverages large language models (LLMs) to generate textual summaries of reinforcement learning (RL) agent policies. The approach involves captioning agent interactions within an environment to convert them into natural language descriptions, which are then used to prompt an LLM to synthesize a comprehensive summary. The paper demonstrates the effectiveness of SySLLM across multiple environments and diverse agent behaviors.  Evaluations include comparisons with RL expert summaries, showing substantial agreement, and a user study comparing SySLLM to a state-of-the-art video summarization technique (HIGHLIGHTS). The user study indicates a preference for SySLLM summaries and shows comparable or superior performance in agent identification tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Novel Approach:**  The use of LLMs for *global* policy summarization in RL is a valuable contribution. Unlike previous methods that rely on demonstrations, SySLLM attempts to distill broader patterns, which is crucial for understanding complex policies.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:**  The paper includes both expert evaluations and a user study, which provides a well-rounded view of the method's performance.
    *   **Clear Methodology:**  The paper clearly explains the SySLLM pipeline, including the captioning process, prompt engineering strategies, and evaluation metrics. The iterative process of developing the prompts seems well executed.
    *   **Practical Relevance:** Explainable RL is crucial for real-world deployment and building trust in AI systems. SySLLM addresses this need by providing accessible, language-based explanations.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Hallucination Risk:**  While the paper addresses the risk of LLM hallucinations, it's a persistent concern. While experts are able to identify some instances of LLM hallucination, it's not clear if the users are as capable. There may be subtle inaccuracies in the generated summaries that skew a user's understanding.
    *   **Captioner Dependence:** The method relies heavily on the quality of the captioner. While the paper mentions the use of a rule-based system, the impact of different captioning strategies isn't thoroughly explored. The dependence on "ground truth states" seems limited to simulation. In real-world applications, captioning might become a bottleneck.
    *   **Limited User Study Scope:** While the user study demonstrates a preference for SySLLM and comparable performance, the agent identification task might not fully capture the depth of policy understanding that summaries are intended to provide.
    *   **Zero-Shot Limitations:** While the authors designed the model to be zero-shot, there still may be some implicit overfitting by the choice of the best-suited prompt. Additionally, the method requires manual adjustment of the prompt for the best possible output.

*   **Significance:**
    *   The paper offers a significant step towards interpretable RL. By effectively using LLMs, SySLLM potentially democratizes access to understanding agent behavior, even for non-experts.
    *   The approach could influence the development of XRL tools and interfaces by providing a basis for creating interactive and queryable agent explanations.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper provides a novel and well-executed methodology for generating natural language summaries of RL agent policies using LLMs. The evaluations are comprehensive, demonstrating both expert agreement and user preferences. The results, showing that users preferred SySLLM summaries, is a significant result. However, the reliance on captioning, the potential for hallucinations, and the limited user study scope slightly detract from the overall impact. Despite these drawbacks, the work represents a substantial contribution to XRL and warrants a high score.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Conformal Prediction Sets for Deep Generative Models via Reduction to Conformal Regression
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10512v1)
- **Authors**: Hooman Shahrokhi, Devjeet Raj Roy, Yan Yan, Venera Arnaoudova, Janaradhan Rao Doppa
- **Abstract**: We consider the problem of generating valid and small prediction sets by sampling outputs (e.g., software code and natural language text) from a black-box deep generative model for a given input (e.g., textual prompt).
