### TruthPrInt: Mitigating LVLM Object Hallucination Via Latent Truthful-Guided Pre-Intervention
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10602v1)
- **Authors**: Jinhao Duan, Fei Kong, Hao Cheng, James Diffenderfer, Bhavya Kailkhura, Lichao Sun, Xiaofeng Zhu, Xiaoshuang Shi, Kaidi Xu
- **Abstract**: Object Hallucination (OH) has been acknowledged as one of the major trustworthy challenges in Large Vision-Language Models (LVLMs). Recent advancements in Large Language Models (LLMs) indicate that internal states, such as hidden states, encode the "overall truthfulness" of generated responses. However, it remains under-explored how internal states in LVLMs function and whether they could serve as "per-token" hallucination indicators, which is essential for mitigating OH. In this paper, we first conduct an in-depth exploration of LVLM internal states in relation to OH issues and discover that (1) LVLM internal states are high-specificity per-token indicators of hallucination behaviors. Moreover, (2) different LVLMs encode universal patterns of hallucinations in common latent subspaces, indicating that there exist "generic truthful directions" shared by various LVLMs. Based on these discoveries, we propose Truthful-Guided Pre-Intervention (TruthPrInt) that first learns the truthful direction of LVLM decoding and then applies truthful-guided inference-time intervention during LVLM decoding. We further propose ComnHallu to enhance both cross-LVLM and cross-data hallucination detection transferability by constructing and aligning hallucination latent subspaces. We evaluate TruthPrInt in extensive experimental settings, including in-domain and out-of-domain scenarios, over popular LVLMs and OH benchmarks. Experimental results indicate that TruthPrInt significantly outperforms state-of-the-art methods. Codes will be available at https://github.com/jinhaoduan/TruthPrInt.
- **Summary**: The paper "TruthPrInt: Mitigating LVLM Object Hallucination Via Latent Truthful-Guided Pre-Intervention" introduces a novel framework, TruthPrInt, designed to reduce object hallucination (OH) in Large Vision-Language Models (LVLMs). The method leverages the internal states of LVLMs to identify per-token hallucination indicators. The authors discover that these internal states encode universal patterns of hallucinations across different LVLMs in common latent subspaces, which they term "generic truthful directions." TruthPrInt learns this truthful direction and then employs truthful-guided inference-time intervention during decoding. Furthermore, the paper introduces ComnHallu, a technique for enhancing cross-LVLM and cross-data hallucination detection transferability by aligning hallucination latent subspaces. Extensive experiments demonstrate that TruthPrInt outperforms state-of-the-art methods on various LVLMs and OH benchmarks, including both in-domain and out-of-domain scenarios.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper demonstrates novelty in several aspects. Firstly, it provides an in-depth exploration of how internal states in LVLMs relate to OH and reveals these states are high-specificity hallucination indicators. Secondly, the discovery of "generic truthful directions" shared across different LVLMs and the design of corresponding intervention methods, TruthPrInt, represents a novel approach to mitigate OH.  The idea of a common hallucination subspace, while intuitively plausible, is well-articulated and empirically supported. ComnHallu also adds value by improving the transferability of hallucination detectors.
*   **Significance:**  Object hallucination is a critical challenge in the development of trustworthy LVLMs, limiting their practical applicability. This paper directly addresses this problem with a theoretically grounded and empirically validated solution. The fact that TruthPrInt demonstrably improves OH mitigation across different models and datasets is highly significant. The discovery about internal states provides valuable insight and may inspire further research on interpretability and control of LVLMs. The gains achieved are noticeable and potentially impactful for real-world deployments.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Generality of Truthful Directions:** While the experiments show promising results, the "truthful directions" might be highly data-dependent, limiting their generalizability beyond the tested benchmarks. More analysis is required to confirm the robustness of the learned directions across a wider range of visual and textual contexts. How sensitive is it to the initial training data?
*   **Practical Deployment Overhead:** Though claimed efficient relative to post-processing methods involving auxiliary LLMs, incorporating TruthPrInt incurs its own computational overhead due to the intervention and latent space calculations. While ablation studies are provided, a more thorough breakdown of runtime performance compared to baselines is desirable to assess practical feasibility, especially for real-time applications. A comparative analysis of floating point operations per second (FLOPS) or inference latency would strengthen this point.
*   **Qualitative Analysis Limitations:** The qualitative examples, while illustrative, provide limited depth of understanding. A more detailed analysis of the types of hallucinations that TruthPrInt successfully mitigates and the types that it struggles with would provide a more nuanced view of its capabilities.
*   **Limited Evaluation on Non-Llama Backbones Initially:** While addressed later with QWen2-VL and InternVL 2.5, the primary experiments focus on Llama-based LVLMs. Expanding and emphasizing the evaluation of the generalizability over diverse backbone architectures from the start would bolster the significance of the findings.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a solid contribution to the field of LVLMs by effectively tackling the important problem of object hallucination. The novelty lies in the insightful analysis of internal states and the design of intervention mechanisms based on learned "truthful directions." The experimental results convincingly demonstrate the superior performance of TruthPrInt. However, the weaknesses related to generality, deployment overhead, and more in-depth qualitative analysis warrant a conservative assessment. The inclusion of experiments showing improvements on models with non-Llama backbones partially addresses the generalizability concerns.

### MuDG: Taming Multi-modal Diffusion with Gaussian Splatting for Urban Scene Reconstruction
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10604v1)
- **Authors**: Yingshuang Zou, Yikang Ding, Chuanrui Zhang, Jiazhe Guo, Bohan Li, Xiaoyang Lyu, Feiyang Tan, Xiaojuan Qi, Haoqian Wang
- **Abstract**: Recent breakthroughs in radiance fields have significantly advanced 3D scene reconstruction and novel view synthesis (NVS) in autonomous driving. Nevertheless, critical limitations persist: reconstruction-based methods exhibit substantial performance deterioration under significant viewpoint deviations from training trajectories, while generation-based techniques struggle with temporal coherence and precise scene controllability. To overcome these challenges, we present MuDG, an innovative framework that integrates Multi-modal Diffusion model with Gaussian Splatting (GS) for Urban Scene Reconstruction. MuDG leverages aggregated LiDAR point clouds with RGB and geometric priors to condition a multi-modal video diffusion model, synthesizing photorealistic RGB, depth, and semantic outputs for novel viewpoints. This synthesis pipeline enables feed-forward NVS without computationally intensive per-scene optimization, providing comprehensive supervision signals to refine 3DGS representations for rendering robustness enhancement under extreme viewpoint changes. Experiments on the Open Waymo Dataset demonstrate that MuDG outperforms existing methods in both reconstruction and synthesis quality.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "MuDG: Taming Multi-modal Diffusion with Gaussian Splatting for Urban Scene Reconstruction" introduces a novel framework that combines a multi-modal diffusion model (MDM) with Gaussian Splatting (GS) for urban scene reconstruction and novel view synthesis (NVS).  The MDM is conditioned on sparse LiDAR point cloud projections to synthesize photorealistic RGB, depth, and semantic videos from novel viewpoints. This synthesized data then acts as a supervisory signal for training the GS model, leading to improved rendering quality and robustness, especially under significant viewpoint deviations from the original training trajectories. The authors demonstrate their approach on the Open Waymo Dataset, showing superior performance compared to existing methods in both reconstruction and synthesis quality. A key feature is the controllability of the model, allowing for scene editing operations like object removal and background modification.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the integration of a controllable multi-modal diffusion model (MDM) *specifically designed and trained* to act as a *feed-forward* supervisory signal for Gaussian Splatting (GS). While both diffusion models and GS are individually established techniques, their synergistic combination in this *specific manner* for addressing the limitations of NVS in urban scenes presents a valuable innovation. The MDM isn't just generating outputs; it's architected to *augment and enhance* the GS representation, which is a novel contribution. The approach addresses the limitations of purely reconstruction-based methods which falter in out-of-distribution viewpoints and generative methods which lack temporal coherence and controllability. The approach of aggregating LiDAR and creating virtual views to augment training data is not entirely new, but the *explicit combination with a specially trained MDM* to create training data *targeted* at improving GS robustness is novel.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from its potential impact on autonomous driving and simulation. The ability to generate high-quality, controllable, and temporally coherent synthetic data offers a cost-effective way to train and evaluate autonomous driving systems, particularly in rare or challenging scenarios. The improvement in NVS quality, especially from viewpoints significantly different from the training data, is crucial for real-world applicability.  The controllability offered by the MDM (scene editing, object removal) is also significant for generating varied and targeted training datasets.  The real-time rendering capability of GS is retained, which makes it practical. While several works also make use of GS in urban environments, this one aims to use a multi-modal generative model to augment the 3DGS optimization. The paper highlights the issue of viewpoint generalization, and then directly approaches it by supervising the GS with virtually rendered views.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Integration:** Cleverly integrates two powerful technologies (diffusion and GS) to overcome their individual limitations.
    *   **Controllability:**  Demonstrates strong controllability over the generated scenes.
    *   **Quantitative Results:**  Provides compelling quantitative results on a challenging dataset (Open Waymo).
    *   **Practicality:** Addresses a practical problem in autonomous driving and simulation.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Complexity:**  The system involves multiple components (MDM, VAE, GS), potentially increasing the complexity of training and deployment. Although end-to-end per scene optimisation is avoided the system is still quite complex.
    *   **Reliance on Pre-trained Models:** The approach relies on pre-trained VAEs, which may introduce biases or limitations. The performance depends on the efficacy of the pre-trained model.
    *   **Limited Generalization:** While the paper demonstrates good results on the Waymo dataset, the generalization capability to other urban environments remains to be fully explored. The authors themselves note an issue with scene boundary artefacts, that is handled by virtue of the blending from the diffusion model, which shows that the system is not completely solved.
    *   **Pseudo Ground Truth:** The reliance on pseudo ground truths for depth and semantics is a potential source of error.

*   **Potential Impact:** The approach has the potential to influence research in autonomous driving simulation, synthetic data generation, and NVS. By demonstrating a practical way to improve the robustness and controllability of 3D scene representations, it can accelerate the development of safer and more reliable autonomous systems.

**Justification of Score:**

I am assigning a score of 7.

*   The paper presents a novel integration of techniques (MDM and GS) with a clear application to a significant problem (autonomous driving).
*   It shows strong quantitative results and demonstrates controllability, supporting its effectiveness.
*   However, the reliance on pre-trained models, the complexity of the system, the remaining issues with the 3DGS representation for certain regions and the generation of pseudo-ground truths, and the need for more extensive generalization studies prevent it from achieving a higher score. The novelty, while present, is an enhancement to existing works not a paradigm shift. This justifies the lower end of the 'good' range (6-8). I lean towards the higher end because the paper demonstrates a thorough understanding of the literature.

### CoSTA$\ast$: Cost-Sensitive Toolpath Agent for Multi-turn Image Editing
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10613v1)
- **Authors**: Advait Gupta, NandaKiran Velaga, Dang Nguyen, Tianyi Zhou
- **Abstract**: Text-to-image models like stable diffusion and DALLE-3 still struggle with multi-turn image editing. We decompose such a task as an agentic workflow (path) of tool use that addresses a sequence of subtasks by AI tools of varying costs. Conventional search algorithms require expensive exploration to find tool paths. While large language models (LLMs) possess prior knowledge of subtask planning, they may lack accurate estimations of capabilities and costs of tools to determine which to apply in each subtask. Can we combine the strengths of both LLMs and graph search to find cost-efficient tool paths? We propose a three-stage approach "CoSTA*" that leverages LLMs to create a subtask tree, which helps prune a graph of AI tools for the given task, and then conducts A* search on the small subgraph to find a tool path. To better balance the total cost and quality, CoSTA* combines both metrics of each tool on every subtask to guide the A* search. Each subtask's output is then evaluated by a vision-language model (VLM), where a failure will trigger an update of the tool's cost and quality on the subtask. Hence, the A* search can recover from failures quickly to explore other paths. Moreover, CoSTA* can automatically switch between modalities across subtasks for a better cost-quality trade-off. We build a novel benchmark of challenging multi-turn image editing, on which CoSTA* outperforms state-of-the-art image-editing models or agents in terms of both cost and quality, and performs versatile trade-offs upon user preference.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "COSTA*: Cost-Sensitive Toolpath Agent for Multi-turn Image Editing".

**Summary:**

The paper introduces COSTA*, a novel agentic mechanism for multi-turn image editing. It addresses the challenge of efficiently performing complex image editing tasks that require multiple steps by combining the strengths of Large Language Models (LLMs) and A* search. COSTA* employs a hierarchical planning strategy. First, it leverages an LLM to decompose the user's request into a subtask tree, representing various possible execution paths. Then, it constructs a tool subgraph based on a tool dependency graph.  Finally, it uses an A* search algorithm on this tool subgraph to find the most cost-effective toolpath, balancing execution cost and output quality according to user-defined preferences.  The approach utilizes a Model Description Table (MDT) and a Benchmark Table (BT) to provide the A* search with prior knowledge about tool capabilities and performance.  A key feature is its ability to learn and adapt from execution failures via a feedback loop that updates tool costs and qualities. The paper also introduces a new benchmark for multi-turn image editing tasks and demonstrates that COSTA* outperforms existing image editing models and agents in terms of both cost and quality.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its intelligent combination of LLM-based planning with a traditional A* search, and the use of explicit cost and quality metrics for tool selection and path optimization. While each component (LLMs for planning, A* search, tool databases) is not novel on its own, their integration to create a cost-sensitive and adaptive image editing agent *is* a significant contribution. The real-time feedback loop for tool cost and quality updating during A* exploration further adds to the approach's novelty.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a practical and increasingly important problem: automating complex image editing workflows. Text-to-image models and existing agents struggle with multi-turn edits. COSTA* aims to provide a more reliable and efficient solution. The introduction of a new benchmark focused on multi-turn editing is a valuable contribution to the community, as existing benchmarks often don't fully capture the challenges of such tasks. The demonstrated ability to achieve Pareto optimality in the cost-quality trade-off has practical implications for real-world applications.

**Strengths:**

*   **Well-defined problem:** Clearly articulates the limitations of existing approaches to multi-turn image editing.
*   **Technically sound approach:** Presents a coherent and well-engineered system that combines multiple techniques.
*   **Comprehensive evaluation:** Thorough experimental results, including comparisons to state-of-the-art methods, ablation studies, and qualitative examples.
*   **New benchmark:**  Provides a valuable resource for the research community.
*   **Practical relevance:** The ability to adaptively adjust the trade-off between cost and quality based on user preferences is highly relevant in real-world scenarios.
*   **Addresses multimodality:** Explicitly handles both image-only and text+image editing tasks, expanding the scope of applicability.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Reliance on Pre-Trained Models:** The approach relies heavily on the performance of pre-trained models, such as the LLM for task decomposition and the various image editing tools used. The overall performance is thus constrained by the quality and biases of these individual components. Although the feedback loop helps to mitigate failures, it cannot entirely overcome inherent limitations of the underlying models.
*   **Limited Scalability Analysis:** While the paper demonstrates improved performance, it would benefit from a more detailed analysis of how the approach scales with even *more* complex tasks (e.g., >8 subtasks).
*   **The feedback/retry mechanism**: The paper could benefit from details regarding manual tuning of the quality threshold for a retry, and what constitutes a good threshold.
*   **Dataset scale:** The dataset, while a nice contribution, contains 121 images for the dataset. This is a small dataset.

**Potential Influence:**

COSTA* has the potential to influence future research in agent-based image editing and automated workflow orchestration. The hierarchical planning approach and the cost-sensitive A* search could inspire new algorithms for tackling other complex tasks in computer vision and related fields. The emphasis on balancing cost and quality and learning from execution failures is a valuable principle for building robust and adaptable AI systems.

**Justification for Score:**

COSTA* presents a significant and novel contribution to the field of image editing by addressing a real-world problem and providing a technically sound and well-evaluated solution. While the approach relies on existing pre-trained models and has some limitations in terms of scalability analysis, the overall concept and results are compelling. The introduction of a new benchmark and the ability to achieve Pareto optimality in the cost-quality trade-off are strong indicators of its potential impact.
I have assigned it a score of 7.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### R1-Onevision: Advancing Generalized Multimodal Reasoning through Cross-Modal Formalization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10615v1)
- **Authors**: Yi Yang, Xiaoxuan He, Hongkun Pan, Xiyan Jiang, Yan Deng, Xingtao Yang, Haoyu Lu, Dacheng Yin, Fengyun Rao, Minfeng Zhu, Bo Zhang, Wei Chen
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models have demonstrated remarkable reasoning capability in complex textual tasks. However, multimodal reasoning, which requires integrating visual and textual information, remains a significant challenge. Existing visual-language models often struggle to effectively analyze and reason visual content, resulting in suboptimal performance on complex reasoning tasks. Moreover, the absence of comprehensive benchmarks hinders the accurate assessment of multimodal reasoning capabilities. In this paper, we introduce R1-Onevision, a multimodal reasoning model designed to bridge the gap between visual perception and deep reasoning. To achieve this, we propose a cross-modal reasoning pipeline that transforms images into formal textural representations, enabling precise language-based reasoning. Leveraging this pipeline, we construct the R1-Onevision dataset which provides detailed, step-by-step multimodal reasoning annotations across diverse domains. We further develop the R1-Onevision model through supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning to cultivate advanced reasoning and robust generalization abilities. To comprehensively evaluate multimodal reasoning performance across different grades, we introduce R1-Onevision-Bench, a benchmark aligned with human educational stages, covering exams from junior high school to university and beyond. Experimental results show that R1-Onevision achieves state-of-the-art performance, outperforming models such as GPT-4o and Qwen2.5-VL on multiple challenging multimodal reasoning benchmarks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the R1-Onevision paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces R1-Onevision, a multimodal reasoning model designed to improve visual perception and reasoning in complex tasks. The approach involves a cross-modal reasoning pipeline that transforms images into formal textual representations, enabling precise language-based reasoning. The authors also contribute the R1-Onevision dataset, a multimodal reasoning dataset with step-by-step annotations across various domains, and R1-Onevision-Bench, a new benchmark aligned with human educational stages.  The model is trained using supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning. Experimental results show that R1-Onevision achieves state-of-the-art performance on several challenging multimodal reasoning benchmarks, surpassing existing models like GPT-40 and Qwen2.5-VL.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of formalizing visual information into a language-based representation isn't entirely novel, as similar concepts have been explored in other areas of multimodal learning and knowledge representation.  However, the specific pipeline for accomplishing this, along with the combination of SFT and RL, might present some incremental innovation. The dataset and benchmark are more significant contributions to the field. The role-playing strategy to enhance coherence is a solid addition.

*   **Significance:** The primary significance of this work lies in the dataset and benchmark contributions.  R1-Onevision Dataset addresses a gap in the existing literature by providing high-quality, step-by-step reasoning annotations.  R1-Onevision-Bench is valuable because it aims to evaluate multimodal reasoning capabilities across different educational levels, which is a more structured approach compared to existing general-purpose benchmarks. If the R1-Onevision benchmark becomes popular within the community it could drive further progress. The experimental results showing improvements over strong baselines suggest the effectiveness of their approach, however there is no analysis on why R1-Onevision excels where it does. The paper should include details on how the R1-Onevision is able to achieve it's scores

*   **Strengths:**

    *   High-quality R1-Onevision Dataset.
    *   The introduction of R1-Onevision-Bench, with its focus on grade-level assessment, is well-motivated and helpful.
    *   Clear presentation of the cross-modal reasoning pipeline.
    *   Strong experimental results showing improved performance.
    *   Use of Reinforcement Learning on multimodal visual tasks is interesting
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The conceptual novelty of the approach is incremental. While the implementation is detailed, the high-level idea is inspired by previous work.
    *   The results are largely measured against existing datasets and not necessarily compared to very advanced models, meaning performance gains may not be as strong. The fact that a GPT-40-mini scoring system was used is also concerning as GPT-40 is already strong.
    *   The paper would benefit from a more in-depth analysis of the model's behavior and failure cases. Understanding *why* R1-Onevision performs better in specific areas is critical.
    * The study lacks ablations or experiments that showcase the importance of the dataset.

*   **Potential Influence:** The R1-Onevision Dataset and R1-Onevision-Bench could serve as valuable resources for the multimodal reasoning research community. This benchmark offers fine-grained analysis on specific tasks. Widespread adoption could standardize evaluation and accelerate progress.

**Score: 6**

**Justification:** R1-Onevision presents a solid contribution with a focus on data and benchmark creation. The cross-modal reasoning pipeline and training strategy (SFT+RL) are practical, but not groundbreaking. The dataset and benchmark, if adopted by the community, could be very valuable. The work could be improved by a deeper dive into the model's capabilities and an exploration of its weaknesses and a stronger ablation strategy. Therefore, the score reflects the positive aspects of the work (datasets/benchmarks, implementation), balanced against the limited conceptual novelty and room for more detailed analysis.


- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### Compositional Subspace Representation Fine-tuning for Adaptive Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10617v1)
- **Authors**: Andy Zhou
- **Abstract**: Adapting large language models to multiple tasks can cause cross-skill interference, where improvements for one skill degrade another. While methods such as LoRA impose orthogonality constraints at the weight level, they do not fully address interference in hidden-state representations. We propose Compositional Subspace Representation Fine-tuning (CS-ReFT), a novel representation-based approach that learns multiple orthonormal subspace transformations, each specializing in a distinct skill, and composes them via a lightweight router. By isolating these subspace edits in the hidden state, rather than weight matrices, CS-ReFT prevents cross-task conflicts more effectively. On the AlpacaEval benchmark, applying CS-ReFT to Llama-2-7B achieves a 93.94% win rate, surpassing GPT-3.5 Turbo (86.30%) while requiring only 0.0098% of model parameters. These findings show that specialized representation edits, composed via a simple router, significantly enhance multi-task instruction following with minimal overhead.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Compositional Subspace Representation Fine-tuning (CS-ReFT), a parameter-efficient fine-tuning method for large language models (LLMs) designed to mitigate cross-task interference in multi-task learning scenarios. CS-ReFT learns multiple orthonormal subspace transformations, each dedicated to a specific skill, and employs a lightweight router to dynamically compose these transformations based on the input. By isolating skill-specific edits within hidden-state subspaces, CS-ReFT aims to prevent conflicts more effectively than weight-based methods. The experiments demonstrate that CS-ReFT achieves state-of-the-art performance on the AlpacaEval benchmark with minimal parameter overhead, surpassing even larger models like GPT-3.5-Turbo.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of using compositional subspaces for parameter-efficient fine-tuning is innovative.  While the paper builds on existing techniques like ReFT and LoRA, it significantly advances the field by explicitly focusing on isolating skills within orthonormal subspaces and composing them via a routing mechanism. The explicit incorporation of orthogonality constraints directly at the hidden state level, rather than weight level as done in orthogonal LoRA variations, is a key distinction. The combination of representation editing with dynamic routing is also a novel contribution.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical challenge in LLM adaptation: cross-task interference. The results on AlpacaEval demonstrate a substantial improvement in performance, which is a significant indicator of the effectiveness of the approach. Achieving better performance than GPT-3.5-Turbo with a tiny fraction of the parameters demonstrates practical significance.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   Strong empirical results demonstrating the effectiveness of CS-ReFT.
    *   Clear and well-motivated approach to address cross-task interference.
    *   Excellent parameter efficiency.
    *   Addresses a very important problem: how to efficiently adapt and combine diverse skills in LLMs.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The paper could benefit from a more in-depth analysis of the router's behavior and the types of skills it learns to compose. While the paper states the router "implicitly" discovers how to route different inputs, further elucidation via interpretability techniques would strenghten the arguments.
    *   The selection of AlpacaEval as the primary benchmark limits the generalizability of the results. While AlpacaEval is a useful benchmark, evaluating on a broader range of multi-task datasets would further validate the robustness of CS-ReFT.
    *   Ablation studies exploring the impact of different router architectures or the number of subspaces would enhance the analysis.
*   **Potential Influence:** CS-ReFT has the potential to influence future research in parameter-efficient fine-tuning and multi-task learning for LLMs. The concept of compositional subspace representation provides a powerful framework for adapting LLMs to diverse tasks while minimizing interference. It is also a potential direction towards modular LLMs, which are more amenable to continuous learning and skill composition.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:**

CS-ReFT presents a novel and significant contribution to parameter-efficient fine-tuning. The concept of isolating skills in orthonormal subspaces and composing them dynamically is both theoretically sound and empirically effective. The performance gains on AlpacaEval are impressive, suggesting that CS-ReFT has the potential to significantly improve the adaptation of LLMs to multi-task scenarios. While the paper could benefit from more detailed analysis and evaluation, its core ideas and results are strong enough to warrant a score of 8. This approach provides a substantial advantage over existing methods for composing diverse skills and offers a promising path towards more flexible and adaptable LLMs.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### DiT-Air: Revisiting the Efficiency of Diffusion Model Architecture Design in Text to Image Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10618v1)
- **Authors**: Chen Chen, Rui Qian, Wenze Hu, Tsu-Jui Fu, Lezhi Li, Bowen Zhang, Alex Schwing, Wei Liu, Yinfei Yang
- **Abstract**: In this work, we empirically study Diffusion Transformers (DiTs) for text-to-image generation, focusing on architectural choices, text-conditioning strategies, and training protocols. We evaluate a range of DiT-based architectures--including PixArt-style and MMDiT variants--and compare them with a standard DiT variant which directly processes concatenated text and noise inputs. Surprisingly, our findings reveal that the performance of standard DiT is comparable with those specialized models, while demonstrating superior parameter-efficiency, especially when scaled up. Leveraging the layer-wise parameter sharing strategy, we achieve a further reduction of 66% in model size compared to an MMDiT architecture, with minimal performance impact. Building on an in-depth analysis of critical components such as text encoders and Variational Auto-Encoders (VAEs), we introduce DiT-Air and DiT-Air-Lite. With supervised and reward fine-tuning, DiT-Air achieves state-of-the-art performance on GenEval and T2I CompBench, while DiT-Air-Lite remains highly competitive, surpassing most existing models despite its compact size.
- **Summary**: This paper, "DiT-Air: Revisiting the Efficiency of Diffusion Model Architecture Design in Text to Image Generation," presents an empirical study and architectural refinement of Diffusion Transformers (DiTs) for text-to-image generation. The authors analyze existing DiT variants like PixArt-style and MMDiT architectures, comparing them to a standard DiT that directly processes concatenated text and noise inputs. Their key finding is that the standard DiT architecture can achieve comparable or superior performance to specialized models, with better parameter efficiency, especially when scaled up. The paper introduces DiT-Air and DiT-Air-Lite, which leverage layer-wise parameter sharing to reduce model size. Experimental results demonstrate state-of-the-art performance on GenEval and T2I CompBench. The authors also conduct a thorough analysis of text encoders and Variational Auto-Encoders (VAEs) to optimize the pipeline.

**Critical Evaluation of Novelty and Significance:**

While the paper presents strong empirical results, its novelty is somewhat incremental. The core idea of revisiting and optimizing DiT architecture is not entirely new. The strength lies in the systematic analysis of different architectural choices, text conditioning strategies, and training protocols. The DiT-Air and DiT-Air-Lite architectures are practical improvements with demonstrated parameter efficiency, but they are not fundamentally new architectural concepts. The layer-wise parameter sharing strategy is borrowed from NLP (ALBERT). The progressive VAE training strategy also is a relatively incremental contribution.  The experiments are well-designed and executed, with comprehensive ablations and comparisons against state-of-the-art models. The improvements in GenEval and T2I CompBench scores are significant, showing the practical value of the proposed approach. However, the reliance on a proprietary dataset and components (text encoders and VAE) is a limitation, making reproducibility difficult and reducing the impact on the broader research community since it's hard for others to directly build upon this work.  The paper’s significance is primarily in demonstrating that a simpler DiT architecture, when properly scaled and trained, can outperform more complex designs in text-to-image generation, challenging the assumption that more complex designs are inherently superior.

In summary, the paper represents a solid engineering effort with valuable empirical findings. However, the lack of significant architectural innovation and the dependence on proprietary resources limit its overall impact on the field.

Score: 6

**Justification:**

The score of 6 reflects the following points:

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Systematic and well-executed empirical study.
    *   Demonstrated parameter efficiency with competitive or state-of-the-art performance.
    *   Comprehensive ablations and comparisons.
    *   Addresses an important problem of efficiency in text-to-image generation.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Incremental architectural novelty.
    *   Borrowing of existing techniques from other domains (NLP, etc.).
    *   Reliance on a proprietary dataset and components limits reproducibility and broader impact.
    *   Some of the improvements, such as the specific layer selection, are likely dataset-specific.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence future research towards simpler and more efficient architectures for text-to-image generation. It highlights the importance of proper scaling and training strategies. However, the proprietary nature of the implementation hinders the ease with which other researchers can adopt and build upon these findings.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### Siege: Autonomous Multi-Turn Jailbreaking of Large Language Models with Tree Search
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10619v1)
- **Authors**: Andy Zhou
- **Abstract**: We introduce Siege, a multi-turn adversarial framework that models the gradual erosion of Large Language Model (LLM) safety through a tree search perspective. Unlike single-turn jailbreaks that rely on one meticulously engineered prompt, Siege expands the conversation at each turn in a breadth-first fashion, branching out multiple adversarial prompts that exploit partial compliance from previous responses. By tracking these incremental policy leaks and re-injecting them into subsequent queries, Siege reveals how minor concessions can accumulate into fully disallowed outputs. Evaluations on the JailbreakBench dataset show that Siege achieves a 100% success rate on GPT-3.5-turbo and 97% on GPT-4 in a single multi-turn run, using fewer queries than baselines such as Crescendo or GOAT. This tree search methodology offers an in-depth view of how model safeguards degrade over successive dialogue turns, underscoring the urgency of robust multi-turn testing procedures for language models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the "Siege: Autonomous Multi-Turn Jailbreaking of Large Language Models with Tree Search" paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Siege, a novel framework for jailbreaking Large Language Models (LLMs) through multi-turn adversarial interactions. Siege employs a breadth-first search (BFS) strategy within a tree-search framework to explore multiple adversarial prompts at each turn in a conversation.  It tracks partial compliance from the LLM's responses and re-injects identified policy leaks into subsequent queries, effectively escalating attacks over time. The authors demonstrate that Siege achieves high success rates on JailbreakBench, surpassing existing multi-turn jailbreaking methods like Crescendo and GOAT in both success rate and efficiency (fewer queries).

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the combination of BFS-style tree search with a partial compliance tracking mechanism in the context of multi-turn jailbreaking.  While multi-turn attacks are not entirely new (Crescendo, GOAT), the systematic exploration of multiple adversarial paths simultaneously, combined with the intelligent exploitation of partial compliance, represents a significant advancement.  Prior work often followed a single conversational path or lacked a robust mechanism for tracking and leveraging incremental policy breaches. The use of tree search to systematically probe the safety boundaries of the LLM is innovative.

*   **Significance:** The work addresses a critical gap in LLM safety evaluations. It highlights the inadequacy of single-turn assessments and demonstrates the real-world threat posed by iterative, adaptive adversarial attacks. By exposing vulnerabilities that arise from the accumulation of seemingly minor concessions, Siege underscores the urgency of developing more robust multi-turn defense mechanisms. The high success rates reported on popular models like GPT-3.5-turbo and GPT-4, coupled with increased efficiency (fewer queries), highlight the practical significance of the work.  The framework also provides a valuable tool for researchers to understand how LLM safeguards degrade over time.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Systematic Approach:** The BFS-style tree search provides a methodical way to explore the adversarial landscape, reducing the reliance on meticulously crafted "magic prompts."
    *   **Partial Compliance Tracking:** The ability to identify and exploit even small policy breaches is a key strength, mimicking real-world attackers' gradual escalation strategies.
    *   **Efficiency:**  The experimental results demonstrate that Siege can achieve high success rates with fewer queries than existing methods, reducing the computational cost of adversarial testing.
    *   **Clear Methodology:** The paper clearly describes the components of the Siege framework, including the attacker LLM, the red-teaming tactics, and the BFS-based search algorithm.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Open-Source Judge Reliance:** The evaluation relies on an open-source judge for assessing partial compliance and attack success. The reliability and potential biases of this judge could impact the validity of the results. An inter-rater reliability analysis by human experts is notably absent.
    *   **Limited Ablation Study:** The paper could benefit from a more in-depth ablation study.  For example, analyzing the impact of the breadth (B) of the search tree and the pruning strategy would be valuable.
    *   **Attacker LLM Configuration**: Details on the Attacker LLM are not robust, thus limiting the repeatability of the experiments.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper will likely influence future research in LLM safety and security. The Siege framework could be adopted by researchers and practitioners for more rigorous adversarial testing. The insights gained from this work may also inform the development of more robust defense mechanisms against multi-turn jailbreaking attacks.

**Justification for Score:**

While multi-turn attacks are not novel, the systematic approach to exploration and the emphasis on exploiting *partial compliance* adds considerable value. While it achieves good performance across a common set of LLMs, the paper's limited ablation studies and reliance on an open-source judge weaken its overall impact.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### From TOWER to SPIRE: Adding the Speech Modality to a Text-Only LLM
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10620v1)
- **Authors**: Kshitij Ambilduke, Ben Peters, Sonal Sannigrahi, Anil Keshwani, Tsz Kin Lam, Bruno Martins, Marcely Zanon Boito, André F. T. Martins
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have shown remarkable performance and generalization capabilities across multiple languages and tasks, making them very attractive targets for multi-modality integration (e.g., images or speech). In this work, we extend an existing LLM to the speech modality via speech discretization and continued pre-training. In particular, we are interested in multilingual LLMs, such as TOWER, as their pre-training setting allows us to treat discretized speech input as an additional translation language. The resulting open-source model, SPIRE, is able to transcribe and translate English speech input while maintaining TOWER's original performance on translation-related tasks, showcasing that discretized speech input integration as an additional language is feasible during LLM adaptation. We make our code and models available to the community.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SPIRE, a speech-augmented large language model (LLM) built upon the existing multilingual model TOWER. The authors aim to integrate speech processing capabilities into TOWER while preserving its original performance on text-based tasks, particularly machine translation.  SPIRE encodes speech using HuBERT-based k-means clustering into discrete speech units (DSUs).  The training process involves two stages: continued pre-training (CPT) on a mixture of text and speech data, and instruction tuning (IT) on task-specific data, including ASR, MT, and ST data. The resulting SPIRE model can perform English speech recognition (ASR) and speech translation (ST) while maintaining TOWER's MT capabilities. The authors demonstrate that both CPT and IT are crucial for optimal speech task performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its attempt to integrate speech into a pre-existing *multilingual* LLM (TOWER) using discretized speech units, specifically focusing on preserving the model's original translation abilities. While other works have explored adding speech to LLMs, this paper's focus on a multilingual setting and the deliberate effort to maintain MT performance differentiate it. The two-stage training approach (CPT + IT) is also not entirely new, but its application in this specific context and its effect on different tasks are worth noting.

*   **Significance:** The paper shows that speech integration into an LLM through discretization is a feasible approach while preserving pre-existing textual task performance. The reported results on ASR and ST, while not surpassing state-of-the-art speech-centric models, are competitive, especially considering the focus on maintaining MT capabilities. The open-source availability of code, models, and datasets contributes to the reproducibility and advancement of research in multimodal LLMs.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem statement and well-defined goals.
    *   Detailed explanation of the methodology, including data preparation, model training, and evaluation.
    *   Comprehensive evaluation across ASR, MT, and ST tasks.
    *   Ablation studies providing insights into the impact of different training stages (CPT and IT).
    *   Focus on multilingual capabilities, extending beyond many existing works in speech-LLM integration.
    *   Code and models made publicly available.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The ASR performance is good but does not surpass specialized models like Whisper or SeamlessM4T. It only outperforms them by a significant margin in the test-clean portion of the Librispeech dataset.
    *   The direct ST results are not particularly strong, suggesting that simply adding speech without dedicated alignment mechanisms between speech and translation is insufficient. It is also unclear, how the results from few-shot prompts compare to the actual results of direct speech translation.
    *   The choice of HuBERT for DSU generation, while commonly used, might not be optimal for all languages or tasks.

*   **Impact and Influence:** The paper contributes to the growing body of research on multimodal LLMs. It could potentially influence future work by:
    *   Encouraging further investigation of discretized speech representation in LLMs.
    *   Highlighting the importance of preserving textual capabilities when integrating other modalities.
    *   Providing a reproducible pipeline for speech-LLM integration.
    *   Serving as a baseline for future multilingual multimodal LLMs.

The paper makes a solid contribution to the field by showing a straightforward way to add a new modality, like speech, to existing LLMs, but the individual ASR/ST results are not particularly groundbreaking and the direct ST capabilities are not great.

**Score: 6.5**

*   The work is definitely a useful contribution, but not revolutionary. Although the direct speech translation scores are good, there is still significant room for improvement and the benefits are not entirely convincing at this stage.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Transformers without Normalization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10622v1)
- **Authors**: Jiachen Zhu, Xinlei Chen, Kaiming He, Yann LeCun, Zhuang Liu
- **Abstract**: Normalization layers are ubiquitous in modern neural networks and have long been considered essential. This work demonstrates that Transformers without normalization can achieve the same or better performance using a remarkably simple technique. We introduce Dynamic Tanh (DyT), an element-wise operation $DyT($x$) = \tanh(\alpha $x$)$, as a drop-in replacement for normalization layers in Transformers. DyT is inspired by the observation that layer normalization in Transformers often produces tanh-like, $S$-shaped input-output mappings. By incorporating DyT, Transformers without normalization can match or exceed the performance of their normalized counterparts, mostly without hyperparameter tuning. We validate the effectiveness of Transformers with DyT across diverse settings, ranging from recognition to generation, supervised to self-supervised learning, and computer vision to language models. These findings challenge the conventional understanding that normalization layers are indispensable in modern neural networks, and offer new insights into their role in deep networks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and rigorous evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Dynamic Tanh (DyT), a simple element-wise operation (DyT(x) = tanh(ax), where 'a' is a learnable parameter) as a direct replacement for normalization layers in Transformers. The authors argue that normalization layers, particularly Layer Normalization (LN), often produce tanh-like input-output mappings, squashing extreme values.  DyT emulates this behavior without computing activation statistics, unlike normalization layers.  The authors demonstrate, across various tasks (recognition, generation, supervised, self-supervised learning, computer vision, language models), that Transformers with DyT can match or exceed the performance of their normalized counterparts, often without hyperparameter tuning.  They challenge the conventional understanding of normalization layers as indispensable components of neural networks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**

    The core idea of replacing normalization layers with a scaled tanh function is somewhat novel. The *observation* that normalization layers produce tanh-like mappings is a good starting point, though not entirely groundbreaking. The proposed DyT layer itself is relatively simple and straightforward to implement. The most novel aspect is the demonstration that such a simple component can effectively replace normalization layers in a variety of transformer architectures.
*   **Significance:**

    The potential significance of this work is considerable if the findings hold up robustly under further scrutiny. Normalization layers are computationally expensive and can introduce complexities in training and inference. A successful replacement like DyT, offering comparable or better performance with less computational overhead, would be a valuable contribution. It could impact efficiency, speed, and potentially even memory footprint for transformer-based models. However, it has some important limitations, namely, it doesn't work well with CNN backbones using Batch Normalization. This needs further research.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Simplicity:** The DyT layer is very simple and easy to implement.
    *   **Broad Empirical Validation:** The paper presents results across a wide range of tasks and architectures, lending credibility to the findings. The fact that it works across vision and language is a strong plus.
    *   **Challenging Convention:** The work directly questions a widely held belief in the necessity of normalization layers.
    *   **Efficiency Claims:** While preliminary, the observation that DyT can improve training and inference speed is promising.
    *   **Analysis:** Attempts to interpret the role of learnable parameter 'a' and demonstrate its relationship to variance helps to explain the proposed method.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Theoretical Justification:** The paper lacks a strong theoretical foundation explaining *why* DyT works.  The claim of tanh-like mapping is primarily empirical. A more rigorous analysis of the optimization landscape or information flow would strengthen the argument.
    *   **Dependency on Alpha tuning:** Experiments show alpha (scaling parameter) initialization is important. Results show that while the method has near-optimal default parameters, significant benefits are obtained by tuning the initialization of the parameter. Further explanation on its convergence is necessary.
    *   **Limited Success with CNN Backbones**: Experiments showed that DyT only works with transformers and not traditional CNNs like ResNet.

*   **Potential Influence:** If validated and refined, this work could influence the design of more efficient and simpler neural network architectures. It could also spur further research into alternatives to normalization layers and a deeper understanding of their mechanisms. It can have a meaningful impact on real-world applications by reducing complexity and improving efficiency.

*   **Justification of Score:**

    Considering both the strengths and weaknesses, the paper is assigned a score of **7**.

    *   The novelty is moderate – the idea is interesting, and simple to implement. The empirical results are comprehensive and compelling enough to warrant further investigation.
    *   The potential significance is high, given the pervasiveness of normalization layers and the potential for efficiency gains.
    *   However, the limited theoretical underpinning and the dependence of hyperparameter tuning weakens the claims. A greater depth of investigation into the 'why' of DyT's effectiveness could have bolstered the findings significantly.
    *   The fact that there is limited success with CNN backbones weakens the claim.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### NIL: No-data Imitation Learning by Leveraging Pre-trained Video Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10626v1)
- **Authors**: Mert Albaba, Chenhao Li, Markos Diomataris, Omid Taheri, Andreas Krause, Michael Black
- **Abstract**: Acquiring physically plausible motor skills across diverse and unconventional morphologies-including humanoid robots, quadrupeds, and animals-is essential for advancing character simulation and robotics. Traditional methods, such as reinforcement learning (RL) are task- and body-specific, require extensive reward function engineering, and do not generalize well. Imitation learning offers an alternative but relies heavily on high-quality expert demonstrations, which are difficult to obtain for non-human morphologies. Video diffusion models, on the other hand, are capable of generating realistic videos of various morphologies, from humans to ants. Leveraging this capability, we propose a data-independent approach for skill acquisition that learns 3D motor skills from 2D-generated videos, with generalization capability to unconventional and non-human forms. Specifically, we guide the imitation learning process by leveraging vision transformers for video-based comparisons by calculating pair-wise distance between video embeddings. Along with video-encoding distance, we also use a computed similarity between segmented video frames as a guidance reward. We validate our method on locomotion tasks involving unique body configurations. In humanoid robot locomotion tasks, we demonstrate that 'No-data Imitation Learning' (NIL) outperforms baselines trained on 3D motion-capture data. Our results highlight the potential of leveraging generative video models for physically plausible skill learning with diverse morphologies, effectively replacing data collection with data generation for imitation learning.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "NIL: No-data Imitation Learning," a novel approach for training physically plausible motor skills for robots with diverse morphologies. NIL leverages pre-trained video diffusion models to generate reference videos of the desired behavior, conditioned on the robot's initial state and a textual description. A reward function, based on video encoding similarity and segmentation mask Intersection over Union (IoU) between the generated video and the robot's simulated behavior, guides the learning process. This method bypasses the need for high-quality expert demonstrations or manual reward engineering, enabling skill acquisition for non-humanoid robots where obtaining such data is difficult. The authors demonstrate the effectiveness of NIL on locomotion tasks with different robot embodiments, showing that it outperforms baselines trained on motion-capture data.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**  The core novelty lies in the integration of pre-trained video diffusion models with imitation learning to create a "data-free" training pipeline. While individual components like video diffusion models and imitation learning are established, their combination to address the specific challenge of skill acquisition for diverse robot morphologies is a significant contribution.  The method offers a way to side-step the reliance on expensive or unavailable expert demonstrations, which is a common bottleneck in robotics and character animation. The use of visual similarity and IoU-based rewards in conjunction with imitation learning also contributes to the novelty.

**Significance:** The significance of this work lies in its potential to democratize skill acquisition for a wider range of robotic platforms. The ability to learn from generated videos, rather than curated datasets, opens up possibilities for training robots with unconventional designs where traditional methods struggle. The experiments demonstrate that NIL achieves competitive results compared to baselines using motion-capture data, highlighting its practical value. Furthermore, the study explores the influence of the used video diffusion model on the final performance which is very useful for understanding the performance characteristics of such combined system and can serve as guide for other works that build on diffusion models for imitation learning.

**Strengths:**

*   **Addresses a key bottleneck:** The method directly tackles the data acquisition problem in robotics.
*   **Generality:** The approach is demonstrated to be applicable to various robot morphologies.
*   **Competitive performance:** The results show that NIL can achieve performance comparable to, and even surpassing, imitation learning methods that use real motion capture data, which is very impressive.
*   **Well-motivated and clearly presented:** The paper is well-written, clearly explains the methodology, and provides comprehensive experiments to support the claims.
*   **Ablation studies:** The ablation studies on the reward function components and diffusion models provide valuable insights into the factors that contribute to the success of NIL.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Dependence on the quality of video diffusion models:** The performance of NIL is inherently limited by the quality and physical realism of the generated videos.  While the authors acknowledge this, further investigation into how to make the system robust to the implausibility of videos would be beneficial.
*   **Computational cost:** Video diffusion models are computationally expensive, which can limit the scalability of the approach.
*   **Limited to relatively simple tasks:** The experiments focus primarily on locomotion. The generalization to more complex manipulation tasks remains to be demonstrated.
*   **Lack of theoretical analysis:** The paper lacks a theoretical understanding of the convergence properties and sample complexity of the proposed algorithm.

**Potential Influence:**

This work has the potential to significantly influence the field of robotics and character animation. It provides a promising alternative to traditional methods for skill acquisition, particularly for robots with unconventional morphologies. The approach may also inspire further research into the integration of generative models with reinforcement learning and imitation learning. Future works could build on NIL by refining the reward function, improving the robustness to unrealistic video artifacts, and exploring its applicability to more complex tasks.

**Justification for the score:**

Despite the identified weaknesses, the novelty and significance of NIL warrant a high score.  The method directly addresses a critical problem in robotics, offers a practical solution, and demonstrates competitive performance. The paper is well-written, thoroughly evaluated, and has the potential to inspire further research in this area. While the approach is still limited by the performance of existing video diffusion models, it represents a significant step forward in data-efficient skill acquisition for robots.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### SciVerse: Unveiling the Knowledge Comprehension and Visual Reasoning of LMMs on Multi-modal Scientific Problems
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10627v1)
- **Authors**: Ziyu Guo, Ray Zhang, Hao Chen, Jialin Gao, Dongzhi Jiang, Jiaze Wang, Pheng-Ann Heng
- **Abstract**: The rapid advancement of Large Multi-modal Models (LMMs) has enabled their application in scientific problem-solving, yet their fine-grained capabilities remain under-explored. In this paper, we introduce SciVerse, a multi-modal scientific evaluation benchmark to thoroughly assess LMMs across 5,735 test instances in five distinct versions. We aim to investigate three key dimensions of LMMs: scientific knowledge comprehension, multi-modal content interpretation, and Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning. To unveil whether LMMs possess sufficient scientific expertise, we first transform each problem into three versions containing different levels of knowledge required for solving, i.e., Knowledge-free, -lite, and -rich. Then, to explore how LMMs interpret multi-modal scientific content, we annotate another two versions, i.e., Vision-rich and -only, marking more question information from texts to diagrams. Comparing the results of different versions, SciVerse systematically examines the professional knowledge stock and visual perception skills of LMMs in scientific domains. In addition, to rigorously assess CoT reasoning, we propose a new scientific CoT evaluation strategy, conducting a step-wise assessment on knowledge and logical errors in model outputs. Our extensive evaluation of different LMMs on SciVerse reveals critical limitations in their scientific proficiency and provides new insights into future developments. Project page: https://sciverse-cuhk.github.io
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SCIVERSE, a new multi-modal benchmark designed to evaluate the scientific knowledge comprehension and visual reasoning abilities of Large Multi-modal Models (LMMs).  SCIVERSE contains 5,735 test instances derived from 1,147 problems spanning physics, chemistry, and biology.  The benchmark addresses three key dimensions of LMM performance in scientific contexts: scientific knowledge comprehension, multi-modal content interpretation, and chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning. The authors generate five versions of each problem, varying the level of included background knowledge (knowledge-free, -lite, -rich) and the distribution of information between text and visual modalities (vision-rich, -only). The paper also introduces a scientific CoT evaluation strategy that assesses the knowledge and logical errors made in LMM reasoning steps. The authors evaluate various open-source and closed-source LMMs, highlighting limitations in scientific proficiency and providing insights for future development.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper possesses a moderate level of novelty. The creation of a new multi-modal dataset is always valuable to the community. The breakdown of scientific problem-solving into the three specific dimensions of knowledge comprehension, multi-modal understanding, and CoT reasoning is useful for fine-grained analysis.  The scientific CoT evaluation strategy is a worthwhile methodological contribution.  Existing scientific QA benchmarks exist (e.g., ScienceQA, MMMU), but SCIVERSE's targeted variations on existing problems, explicitly designed to evaluate those dimensions of scientific problem-solving are where this work finds it's strength.

**Significance and Impact:**

The significance of SCIVERSE lies in its potential to provide a more granular understanding of the strengths and weaknesses of LMMs in scientific domains. It is a significant step towards moving beyond simplistic accuracy metrics in evaluating these models.  The insights gained from using this benchmark could inform future research directions, leading to improved LMM architectures and training strategies specifically tailored for scientific applications. It provides a framework for researchers to diagnose the *types* of errors LMMs make when engaging with scientific problems. This is particularly crucial as LMMs are increasingly being explored as tools for scientific discovery and education.

**Strengths:**

*   **Well-defined problem:** Clearly identifies and tackles limitations in existing benchmarks.
*   **Comprehensive dataset design:** The multi-version approach allows for targeted assessment of specific LMM capabilities.
*   **Rigorous CoT evaluation strategy:** Provides a more nuanced assessment of reasoning abilities compared to simple answer accuracy.
*   **Extensive evaluation:**  The evaluation of a wide range of LMMs provides a good overview of the current landscape.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Reliance on Existing Problems:**  The work draws on existing QA datasets rather than creating questions from scratch.  While beneficial for building a dataset rapidly, there is a degree of overlap with existing assessments and thus some questions regarding uniqueness are raised.
*   **Annotation Dependence:** The CoT evaluation relies on GPT-40, which is itself an LMM and can be imperfect. While this offers an objective assessment, error propagation from GPT-40 to the error assessment of the LMMs might be possible and should be considered.
*   **Limited Scope:** While covering a range of scientific topics, it's not exhaustive. Future work could expand the scope to include more specialized domains. The focus is limited to multiple-choice questions.  Real-world scientific problem-solving often involves more open-ended tasks.

**Justification for Score:**

SCIVERSE represents a solid contribution to the field.  It improves upon existing benchmarks by providing a more fine-grained analysis of LMM capabilities in scientific contexts. The insights gained from its unique design could be valuable for guiding future LMM development, but the core novelty is somewhat incremental rather than revolutionary. The reliance on existing benchmarks and the GPT-40 annotation also present limitations that affect the overall impact score.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Uncertainty in Action: Confidence Elicitation in Embodied Agents
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10628v1)
- **Authors**: Tianjiao Yu, Vedant Shah, Muntasir Wahed, Kiet A. Nguyen, Adheesh Juvekar, Tal August, Ismini Lourentzou
- **Abstract**: Expressing confidence is challenging for embodied agents navigating dynamic multimodal environments, where uncertainty arises from both perception and decision-making processes. We present the first work investigating embodied confidence elicitation in open-ended multimodal environments. We introduce Elicitation Policies, which structure confidence assessment across inductive, deductive, and abductive reasoning, along with Execution Policies, which enhance confidence calibration through scenario reinterpretation, action sampling, and hypothetical reasoning. Evaluating agents in calibration and failure prediction tasks within the Minecraft environment, we show that structured reasoning approaches, such as Chain-of-Thoughts, improve confidence calibration. However, our findings also reveal persistent challenges in distinguishing uncertainty, particularly under abductive settings, underscoring the need for more sophisticated embodied confidence elicitation methods.
- **Summary**: This paper introduces Elicitation Policies and Execution Policies to improve confidence elicitation in embodied AI agents operating in complex, open-ended environments like Minecraft. The Elicitation Policies structure confidence assessment based on inductive, deductive, and abductive reasoning, while the Execution Policies enhance calibration by reinterpreting scenarios and sampling actions. The authors evaluate the agents on calibration and failure prediction tasks, demonstrating that structured reasoning approaches (e.g., Chain-of-Thoughts) improve confidence calibration, although challenges remain in distinguishing uncertainty, especially in abductive settings. The work systematically analyzes embodied uncertainty and identifies methods for improvement, highlighting persistent challenges in accurately assessing agent confidence.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its systematic investigation of confidence elicitation in embodied AI, specifically within the context of open-ended, multimodal environments like Minecraft. While confidence elicitation is a well-studied area in traditional machine learning, its application to embodied agents navigating complex environments is relatively unexplored. The introduction of Elicitation and Execution Policies is a novel contribution, providing a structured framework for addressing uncertainty in these settings. Furthermore, the analysis of inductive, deductive, and abductive reasoning in relation to confidence elicitation is a unique perspective.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial challenge in embodied AI: ensuring that agents can accurately assess and communicate their uncertainty. This is vital for building reliable and trustworthy AI systems, especially in high-stakes applications. The findings offer valuable insights into the factors that influence agent confidence and provide practical strategies for improving calibration. The focus on open-ended environments like Minecraft is also significant, as it represents a more realistic and challenging testbed compared to simpler, more controlled environments. However, the evaluation is limited to Minecraft, and the generalizability of the findings to other embodied AI domains could be further explored.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   The paper presents a clear and well-defined problem.
    *   The proposed Elicitation and Execution Policies offer a structured approach to confidence elicitation in embodied AI.
    *   The experimental evaluation provides empirical evidence of the effectiveness of the proposed methods.
    *   The paper identifies persistent challenges in embodied confidence elicitation, highlighting areas for future research.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The evaluation is limited to the Minecraft environment, raising questions about the generalizability of the findings.
    *   The reliance on large pre-trained models raises concerns about computational cost and ethical considerations.
    *   The paper could benefit from a more in-depth analysis of the limitations of current language models in expressing uncertainty.

Overall, the paper represents a valuable contribution to the field of embodied AI. It introduces a novel framework for confidence elicitation in complex environments, provides empirical evidence of its effectiveness, and identifies important challenges for future research.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:**

While the paper offers a novel approach to an important problem, its novelty is somewhat incremental, building upon existing work in confidence elicitation and embodied AI. The experimental evaluation is thorough but limited in scope. A stronger score would require demonstrating generalizability across various enviornments. Nevertheless, the structured approach and the systematic analysis are significant contributions, justifying a score of 7.5. The study offers valuable insights and will likely inspire further research in embodied confidence elicitation.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### UniGoal: Towards Universal Zero-shot Goal-oriented Navigation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10630v1)
- **Authors**: Hang Yin, Xiuwei Xu, Lingqing Zhao, Ziwei Wang, Jie Zhou, Jiwen Lu
- **Abstract**: In this paper, we propose a general framework for universal zero-shot goal-oriented navigation. Existing zero-shot methods build inference framework upon large language models (LLM) for specific tasks, which differs a lot in overall pipeline and fails to generalize across different types of goal. Towards the aim of universal zero-shot navigation, we propose a uniform graph representation to unify different goals, including object category, instance image and text description. We also convert the observation of agent into an online maintained scene graph. With this consistent scene and goal representation, we preserve most structural information compared with pure text and are able to leverage LLM for explicit graph-based reasoning. Specifically, we conduct graph matching between the scene graph and goal graph at each time instant and propose different strategies to generate long-term goal of exploration according to different matching states. The agent first iteratively searches subgraph of goal when zero-matched. With partial matching, the agent then utilizes coordinate projection and anchor pair alignment to infer the goal location. Finally scene graph correction and goal verification are applied for perfect matching. We also present a blacklist mechanism to enable robust switch between stages. Extensive experiments on several benchmarks show that our UniGoal achieves state-of-the-art zero-shot performance on three studied navigation tasks with a single model, even outperforming task-specific zero-shot methods and supervised universal methods.
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and evaluation of the paper "UniGoal: Towards Universal Zero-shot Goal-oriented Navigation."

**Summary**

The paper presents UniGoal, a framework designed for universal zero-shot goal-oriented navigation. The core idea is to provide a single, unified solution that can handle different goal types (object category, instance image, and text description) without requiring task-specific training. UniGoal employs a uniform graph representation for both the scene and the goal. This enables LLMs to perform explicit graph-based reasoning. The framework consists of:

1.  Constructing an online scene graph from observations.
2.  Matching the scene graph with the goal graph at each time step.
3.  Implementing a multi-stage scene exploration policy based on the matching state:
    *   Iterative subgraph searching when the matching is zero.
    *   Coordinate projection and anchor pair alignment for partial matching.
    *   Scene graph correction and goal verification for perfect matching.

A blacklist mechanism prevents the agent from repeatedly exploring regions that have already proven unsuccessful. The authors demonstrate the effectiveness of UniGoal on several benchmarks, showing it achieves state-of-the-art zero-shot performance across multiple navigation tasks.

**Rigorous and Critical Evaluation**

**Novelty:**

*   The key novelty lies in the *unified representation* of goals and scenes using graphs, allowing a single inference pipeline for various goal types.  This contrasts with previous methods that were often tailored to specific task types or used text-based representations that might lose structural information.
*   The *multi-stage exploration policy* is also a significant contribution.  It adaptively changes the exploration strategy based on the degree of matching between the scene and goal graphs, making it more efficient and robust.
*   The *blacklist mechanism* enhances robustness by avoiding repeatedly matching unsuccessful regions.

**Significance:**

*   The paper addresses a significant challenge in robotics: creating versatile agents that can handle different types of goals in unknown environments.
*   Zero-shot navigation is a challenging problem, and the paper demonstrates a viable solution using LLMs and graph-based reasoning. This moves beyond simple use of LLMs and incorporates structural scene understanding.
*   The performance improvements on multiple benchmarks relative to existing zero-shot and even supervised universal methods demonstrate the practicality and potential of the approach.

**Strengths:**

*   **Unified Approach:** The main strength is the ability to handle different goal types within a single framework without task-specific training.
*   **Graph-Based Reasoning:**  Using graph representations allows for explicit structural reasoning.
*   **Performance:** UniGoal achieves state-of-the-art or competitive performance on multiple benchmarks, demonstrating its effectiveness.
*   **Clear Explanations:** The paper does a good job explaining the components of the framework and the rationale behind them.
*   **Ablation Studies:** The ablation studies provide insight into the contribution of each component.

**Weaknesses:**

*   The description of the graph construction process and LLM prompting could be clearer and more detailed in the main text. While the supplementary material likely has more details, some core aspects seem glossed over in the main paper.
*   The complexity of the framework, with multiple stages and mechanisms, might make it challenging to implement and debug.  More detailed implementation details would be valuable.
*   The reliance on LLMs also introduces potential limitations regarding computational resources and the stability/consistency of LLM outputs. The sensitivity to LLM prompts is a concern.
*   The paper compares to a range of approaches, but a more in-depth analysis of the limitations of each baseline and *why* UniGoal addresses those limitations would be useful.
*   The long-term scaling to more complex tasks is not evaluated.

**Justification of Score**

UniGoal presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of robotic navigation. While there are some weaknesses regarding implementation details and scalability, the unified representation and multi-stage exploration policy demonstrate a promising approach for universal zero-shot goal-oriented navigation. The method achieves competitive results on established benchmarks. The integration of LLMs with graph-based structures for scene understanding is an important step forward. The paper presents a reasonable approach to a hard problem, but the approach also seems complicated in its implementation.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10
